Massachusetts AG issues rule on shared appreciation mortgages, NCLC calls for broader protections

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Massachusetts Attorney General’s Office has implemented a new rule governing shared appreciation mortgages, a dual-lien structure that ties repayment to home price appreciation. The regulation clarifies when nonprofit lenders are shielded from consumer protection claims and requires stronger disclosures, including maximum potential repayment amounts. Stemming from a 2024 law that granted broad liability waivers to nonprofit SAMs such as BlueHub, the rule follows litigation alleging the products function as high-cost, subprime-style loans that can strip home equity.   AI Summary

The Massachusetts Attorney General’s Office has implemented a new rule governing shared appreciation mortgages, adding disclosure and consumer protection requirements for a niche home equity product that has faced recent scrutiny.

The rule, effective Nov. 6, clarifies when nonprofit shared appreciation mortgage (SAM) lenders are shielded from state consumer protection claims and sets new notice standards, including prominent disclosure of the maximum amount borrowers may have to repay, according to an announcement from the National Consumer Law Center (NCLC).

The regulation stems from a provision in the state’s economic development bill, passed in November 2024. That statute carved out a broad waiver of liability under Massachusetts consumer protection laws for certain nonprofit SAMs, including loans originated by BlueHub Capital. Advocates say the legislative change created a “dangerous loophole” that exposed distressed homeowners to complex and costly loan structures with limited recourse.

Under the BlueHub-style model, borrowers typically receive a fixed-rate first mortgage alongside a second lien tied to future home price appreciation. The SAM second lien requires the homeowner to share a portion of any increase in property value with the lender upon sale, transfer or refinance. Because the payoff amount is variable and not set in dollars at origination, borrowers can face unexpectedly large balloon payments when they attempt to exit the loan.

NCLC noted that the risks of these structures have been highlighted in Humes v. BlueHub Capital Inc., a Massachusetts class-action lawsuit alleging SAMs embed hidden, subprime-level costs that can strip significant equity and accelerate home loss. The court found BlueHub liable for violating multiple state lending laws and recently certified the class to move forward, according to NCLC.

“While presented as a lifeline, shared appreciation mortgages often become a trap,” Andrea Bopp Stark, senior attorney at the National Consumer Law Center, said in a statement. “The AG’s final rule protects people from BlueHub’s loan model and similar products, but Massachusetts must go further to prevent harm from more common home equity theft loan products, including Home Equity Investment Loans.”

The AG’s new rule does not eliminate SAMs but seeks to ensure that nonprofit and other lenders using these instruments do so transparently and within defined consumer protection boundaries.

The regulation spells out the limited circumstances in which a SAM lender can be insulated from claims under Massachusetts consumer protection law and establishes a framework for robust disclosures, including clear presentation of the maximum possible repayment obligation.

While praising the change, NCLC argues that the rule does not reach a far enough to impact a growing category of products — home equity investments (HEIs). These provide upfront cash to a homeowner in exchange for a share of the future home price appreciation. NCLC characterized these arrangements as high risk for borrowers, as the actual amount owed depends on home price growth and triggers large, lump-sum obligations.

A representative at the AG’s office did not immediately respond to HousingWire‘s request for comment on the changes.

Massachusetts AG Andrea Joy Campbell has already challenged one HEI provider, Hometap. The state alleges consumer protection violations tied to HEI structures. But NCLC noted that the latest rulemaking in Massachusetts does not specifically regulate HEI products.

“The new rules provide important safeguards and requirements for SAMs, and stronger and broader rules are needed to address the high-risk HEI loans that are now targeting Massachusetts homeowners,” Bopp Stark said.

Consumers in other parts of the country have challenged HEI providers in court. Last year, the Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals overturned a 2023 judgment from a district court in Washington state, ruling that Unison‘s HEI product amounted to a reverse mortgage under state law.

In August, HEI provider Unlock settled with the Office of the Minnesota Attorney General over allegations that the company’s home equity agreements (HEAs) were mortgage loans that violated state usury caps, disclosure rules and licensing requirements.

Companies say they are supportive of regulatory efforts, pointing to a legal framework passed earlier this year in Illinois that could serve as model at the national level. At the Mortgage Bankers Association‘s Annual Convention & Expo last month, executives at Point told HousingWire they believe universal regulations are coming.

“We believe that these companies are going to be regulated,” said Dan McPheeters, Point’s head of business development and partnerships. “The practical effect is, it’s difficult to comply with [mortgage regulations] for a product that doesn’t have a monthly payment, that isn’t self-amortizing. … That said, as long as you address those tactical issues, this should be regulated.”

The NCLC urges additional state action to close what they see as remaining gaps in oversight of shared appreciation equity-based mortgage products. Their recommendations include:

powerhouse parramatta’s steel-latticed museum opens with art by es devlin and james turrell

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

behind its intricate white exoskeleton, the sydney museum welcomes its first visitors with an ambitious program of exhibitions.

Powerhouse Parramatta, the new museum designed by Moreau Kusunoki and Genton in Western Sydney, Australia, will officially open to the public on November 7th, 2026. Following years of construction along the Parramatta River, the monumental cultural building, with its lattice facade, is ready to welcome its first visitors, with five inaugural exhibitions including immersive installations by Es Devlin and James Turrell.

The opening marks a significant moment for Western Sydney, bringing the region its first state cultural institution and the largest museum ever built in New South Wales. Spanning more than 18,000 square meters of public and exhibition space, the building introduces a new home for the Powerhouse’s collections of art, design, science, and technology. More than 3,000 objects will be presented across the opening exhibitions, with one designed by OMA/AMO.

The museum‘s distinctive steel exoskeleton has already established a striking presence along the Parramatta River. Designed by French and Japanese architecture studio Moreau Kusunoki with Australian practice Genton, the building comprises two large volumes enclosed by a white structural lattice. Its outward-facing framework allows for expansive, column-free interiors, giving the museum flexibility to accommodate installations of varying scales.

Beyond the galleries, the project introduces new public spaces along the waterfront, including a landscaped square connecting the museum to the river. A rooftop garden, observatory, and spaces for educational programming extend its use beyond conventional exhibition halls. With the building now complete, attention turns to the artists and collections that will occupy its vast interiors.

Among the opening presentations is The Turning, the first major Australian exhibition by British artist Es Devlin. Known for her large-scale installations and stage designs, Devlin brings together five works exploring the relationships between human perception, collective memory, and the natural world.

The exhibition includes Forest of Us, a mirrored labyrinth that draws parallels between the branching structures of human lungs and trees, and Memory Palace, a three-dimensional landscape tracing moments that have shaped human understanding across 75,000 years. In Collective Portrait, visitors are invited to sit opposite strangers and draw one another, contributing to an evolving installation. Other works explore drawing and the biodiversity of the Sydney region, inviting audiences to take an active role in the exhibition.

American artist James Turrell also contributes to the opening program with Shangri La (Over The Hump), a monumental light installation presented as part of Task Eternal, an exhibition examining humanity’s fascination with flight and space exploration.

The work belongs to Turrell’s celebrated Ganzfeld series, which uses light and color to alter the viewer’s perception of space. Visitors enter an environment of shifting hues, where the horizon appears to disappear and familiar boundaries become difficult to distinguish. The installation draws on Turrell’s longstanding research into human vision and sensory experience, offering an encounter with the uncertainty of depth and distance.

Elsewhere, the opening program includes The Mall, developed with OMA/AMO to examine the cultural history of shopping centers, and Overflow, a First Nations-led exhibition exploring relationships with water across New South Wales. An immersive children’s exhibition, The Dark, completes the five inaugural presentations.

The museum’s public opening also begins a 23-day festival of performances, workshops, and conversations, bringing activity into the galleries and surrounding spaces. Together, the exhibitions offer the first opportunity to experience Powerhouse Parramatta as a functioning cultural institution, with its architecture now serving the artists, collections, and communities it was designed to accommodate.

name: Powerhouse Parramatta | @powerhousemuseum lead designer: Moreau Kusunoki | @moreau.kusunoki local architect: Genton engineering: Arup location: Parramatta, Sydney, Australia

9 players Fergie decided not to sign for Man Utd: Lampard, Silva, Zidane…

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sir Alex Ferguson signed some wonderful players during his 27 years in charge of Manchester United – but he missed out on a fair few, too.

Ferguson didn’t get too many things wrong at Old Trafford. But as well as several inspired decisions among around 100 signings, there were also a few duds.

However, as much as Fergie may regret some of the players he signed over the years, he surely regrets some of those he didn’t sign just as much.

No United fan would criticise Ferguson for putting Eric Cantona above any potential new recruit, but the prospect of Zidane at Old Trafford is too tantalising not to regret.

Martin Edwards, in his book, lifted the lid on Ferguson’s thinking when it was put to him that United should be signing the Frenchman from Bordeaux in 1996: “Alex said that Eric had also mentioned Zidane to him, but Alex felt Zidane played in the same position as Eric.

“Having gone over to France to persuade Eric to re-sign for us, after the Crystal Palace incident, he felt that, if he had brought Zidane in, it may have affected Eric’s position, so he stuck with Eric.”

As it turned out, Cantona retired just one season after Zidane left Bordeaux for Juventus.

READ: A forensic analysis of Zizou’s performance vs Brazil, 2006 World Cup

Liverpool don’t have much to thank Ferguson for, but passing up the chance to sign John Barnes from Watford in 1987 is certainly one reason to be grateful.

Edwards explains in his autobiography, Red Glory: ”Graham Taylor rang Alex Ferguson to see if he wanted to take him before he signed for Liverpool.

“Alex passed on him because it was his first season at United, he had Jesper Olsen in his squad and he wanted to see whether Jesper would make it.”

Olsen showed glimpses, but the Denmark winger had nothing even remotely like the impact Barnes made on Liverpool.

Even the staunchest City-hating United fan will admit – privately, at least – that it is impossible to find any reason not to love David Silva. But Ferguson managed it.

Before the Spanish genius joined Manchester City in 2010 for £24million, United had their eye on him.

“We watched Silva a lot when he was at Valencia,” said Ferguson. “My problem with that was you have to be very, very good to play in that position, because there’s no defensive duties for them.

“So if you’re wanting to be really successful, everyone has to do their turn, has to work, you know?”

Roberto Mancini, Manuel Pellegrini and Pep Guardiola all managed to get Silva to do the dirty work. Maybe all you had to do was ask?

For David Silva’s birthday, one of the most satisfying passes you’ll ever see 📬

— B/R Football (@brfootball) January 8, 2022

The likes of Bryan Robson, Roy Keane and Paul Scholes all starred in midfield for United and famously had an eye for goal.

Ferguson also saw that in Lampard before he moved to Chelsea in 2001, and the ex-United boss acknowledges he might have made a mistake by not following through with his initial interest.

“Lampard has been unbelievable,” said Ferguson. “I don’t think a midfield player will be able to do that figure again. It’s quite phenomenal.

“Like Bryan Robson, he has had a great knack of timing his runs into the box at the right time. John Wark was another one at Ipswich.

“The guy has had a great career. I must say we looked at him when he was at West Ham as a young player and I maybe regret not having done it.

Not from Juan Sebastian Veron, who moved to Old Trafford at the same time Lampard went to Chelsea, that’s for certain.

Despite signing Peter Schmeichel, Edwin van der Sar and David de Gea during his reign at Old Trafford, Ferguson occasionally had a blind spot when it came to goalkeepers.

Replacing Schmeichel took six years, and during that time, Cech was making a very positive impression in Ligue 1.

“I went to see him in 2003 for Rennes,” Ferguson said. “We thought Petr was a bit young for the bruisers in the Premier League.”

So he persisted with Tim Howard and Roy Carroll passing the gloves between them and all-too-often dropping them.

Cech went to Chelsea instead in 2004, where he had over a decade of success, but Van der Sar arrived a year later and he didn’t do too badly either.

Cech wasn’t the only Chelsea legend who Ferguson dithered over. Drogba was also catching the eye in France with Marseille, but the United boss’s uncertainty allowed the Blues almost a free run.

“We went to check him out [at Marseille] but the club wanted £25million and Chelsea moved in for the kill before we had made up our minds,” Ferguson said.

A month later, Ferguson splashed £27million on Wayne Rooney, and that worked out okay in the end…

“His agent was demanding a price we were not prepared to pay,” said Ferguson when asked about United’s interest in Sergio Aguero.

Whatever amount it was, it would almost certainly have been worth it.

Less than a year after Aguero joined Manchester City for what turned out to be a total of £31.5million, the striker scored the last-gasp goal that denied Ferguson and United the title in 2012.

🗣️ "Manchester City are still alive here..

I swear you will never see anything like this again.

9 years ago today, Sergio Aguero scored THAT goal against QPR 🔥

Raul was 33 when United had the chance to sign him after he left Real Madrid, but given Ferguson opted against doing so because he had Michael Owen on his books, he may regret his decision.

“Bringing in Raul would have been similar to when we managed to get Henrik Larsson for a short time,” Ferguson said back in 2011.

“We spoke to his agent about a possible move but we already had Javier Hernandez by then and, with Wayne Rooney, Dimitar Berbatov and Michael Owen available, we thought we could afford to let the chance pass.

“If we hadn’t had Michael at the club at the time I might well have signed him.”

Owen scored two goals in just one start and 10 substitute appearances in the Premier League that season. Raul became a cult hero at Schalke.

Just 45 minutes was all it took for Ferguson to decide that Ribery wasn’t good enough for Manchester United.

Alistair Campbell, the former Labour Party spokesman, tells the story of when he accompanied Ferguson to watch Bolton play Marseille, featuring a 22-year-old Ribery.

“Alex was watching Franck Ribery, the Marseille winger but decided by half-time that he was not good enough for United,” he said.

Not good enough for United, but Bayern Munich reckoned otherwise and it seems they were right.

The Cervélo Soloist—The Race-Inspired Road Bike That Makes Sense for the Way Most Riders Actually Ride

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A race-inspired road bike built for riders who want one fast machine for climbs, descents, and pacelines.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. How we test gear.

Over the last few years, many bike brands have tried to combine the benefits of their aero bike platform and climbing bikes into an all-arounder. When I managed a Trek store, the brand launched the Madone Gen8. Trek intended it as the next logical step in its road bike lineup by combining the Madone’s speed with the Émonda’s low weight. Other examples of this trend include the Specialized Tarmac and Cannondale’s SuperSix Evo.

Cervélo touts the Soloist as its do-everything road platform. While you’ll see its S5 and R5 at races like the Tour de France, the Soloist probably won’t show up at the starting line of any Grand Tours. That’s because the Cervélo-sponsored Visma-Lease a Bike team doesn’t need a third road bike; it already has race-winning aero bikes and dedicated climbing bikes for its athletes. Most days those riders use the S5, and on the steepest alpine climbs they can go with the R5.

But most of us don’t have the money, space, or desire to own multiple road bikes to choose between depending on the route we’re riding that day. One road bike that flies up climbs, inspires confidence on descents, and holds well in a paceline is the key for many riders. After almost three months of riding, I found Cervélo’s Soloist fits right into that sweet spot.

The Soloist mixes the pure speed of the S5 with the uphill float of the R5. It sits between both models in weight—about a pound heavier than the R5 and about two pounds lighter than the S5. Visually, the Soloist looks like a midpoint between both bikes. It brings the lines of the R5 while tying in the aero aesthetics of the S5, but the geometry charts paint a different picture. The Soloist leans toward the S5 when looking at the geometry charts for each model. When shopping the Cervélo lineup consider the pricing. The Ultegra Di2 model of the S5 and R5 are both listed at $10,100. The Soloist gives the benefits of each bike, and saves you almost $2,000 for the Ultegra Di2 model.

The Soloist lineup ranges from $6,800 for the SRAM Rival AXS model up to $11,000 for the SRAM Red AXS–equipped bike. I tested the Shimano Ultegra Di2-equipped version, which sells for $8,250. This bike comes stock with a 4iii power meter, an integrated carbon bar and stem, and carbon wheels. My 54cm test bike weighed 15.9 pounds without the bottles or the pedals—pretty respectable for an aero-ish bike with Ultegra components.

Straight out of the box, it was almost ready to roll. Although Cervélo equips this bike with many parts I’d choose for my own bike, I swapped out the stock 700x29c Vittoria Corsa Next TLR G2.0 tires after only a few rides. The rubber was okay on flatter rides, but lacked the grip and ride feel I wanted on twisting descents.

Installing 28mm Pirelli P Zero TLR Race tires was exactly what the Soloist needed to elevate my confidence on descents. The tires made the bike feel locked-in when leaning into turns (I even bagged a couple of Strava trophies on our local descents).

The Soloist ships with aero bottles. Cervélo claims the bottles save 4.3 watts compared to standard round bottles. However, that savings doesn’t matter if you’re wasting energy fumbling with your bottles. The aero ones fit so tight in their cages I almost fell on my first ride trying to remove one.

Even after weeks of testing, I didn’t get used to these bidons. I’ve tried other aero bottle setups and haven’t had the same issue. Plus, the stock aero cages that come with the bike don’t pair as well with traditional round bottles as some brands’ aero cages.

For my first ride, I dove right into the deep end by taking the Soloist up Fiddler’s Elbow. This notorious mile-and-a-half climb gains a little over 700 feet with grades that exceed 20 percent near its summit.

I’ve done this climb at least twenty times on my Trek Madone, with gearing that is a bit more favorable for climbing than the Soloist’s 36x34t lowest gear. But even with taller gearing, the Cervélo floated up the climb. The bike’s geometry put me in a comfortable position, and I appreciated the Soloist’s lower weight on steeper parts of the climb.

But the Soloist didn’t only excel on climbs. The backside of Fiddler’s features a twisting, high-speed descent where maintaining your line is critical, and the bike felt stable. Once I reached the bottom, I had rolling farm roads back to the office, similar to where I did the bulk of my daily rides with this bike.

The Soloist shines on undulating rural roads and group rides. Sometimes our office lunch rides lean toward a spicier pace, with a single or double paceline of up to eight riders. The Soloist is calm, collected, and easy to ride in groups, and its aerodynamics helped when I started to fall off the back and had to catch back to the group.

Lastly, since you don’t always ride on butter-smooth tarmac, I also tested the Soloist on roads outside of its typical scope. The bike felt confident, even on some of the chunky gravel roads. However, I don’t recommend doing this too often with the Soloist; it doesn’t have enough tire clearance for actual gravel tread.

The bike proved easy to ride and live with. I wish I didn’t have to send the Soloist back to Cervélo, as I’d gladly spend more hours on this bike.

Bicycling’s Test Editor, Dan Chabanov, felt like the previous model tried to mix the benefits of the R5 and S5, but missed the mark. “I found it pretty unremarkable. Not in a bad way, but compared to other bikes at the time it just kind of split everything down the middle. It wasn’t very aero but it was aero-ish. It wasn’t very light, but it wasn’t overly heavy. It did a lot of stuff well, but it never really wowed me,” says Chabanov.

The new model irons out those wrinkles. Cervélo shaved nearly a pound off the Ultegra model and added more aero features from the S5—notably the integrated handlebar and stem combination.

I’m impressed after a few months using the new-generation Soloist as my primary road bike. It climbs better than other comparable bikes I’ve tried, holds a pocket in a paceline well, and descends like a banshee. Many brands have tried to blend the watt savings of an aero bike and the weight of a climbing bike. Cervélo took a swing with the previous Soloist model, but refined the previous generation to create a well-blended machine.

This bike is the great choice for everyone from the local crit ripper to the Saturday coffee shop ride champion. If you get this bike, then pick up a pair of Pirelli P Zero Race tires or Continental GP 5000 tires to unlock its full potential. This bike is at the top of my list for those of us who want to have one road bike at home that can rip every ride.

Jason Griffin is Bicycling's Senior Health and Fitness Editor, bringing more than a decade of fitness experience and years in the cycling industry to every story. A former personal trainer and bike shop manager, he's lined up for crits, tackled enduros, but prefers getting lost on multi-day bikepacking adventures. Lately though, his toughest balancing act has been fitting trainer rides between family life and the next adventure.

Former England defender Conor Coady has announced his retirement - a year on from nearly joining Rangers.

Coady was a transfer target for Rangers under Russell Martin in the summer of 2025 after leaving Leicester City, but instead move to English Championship side Wrexham.

The 33-year-old started his career at Liverpool, coming through the youth ranks to make two senior appearances, before spells at Huddersfield, Wolves, Everton, Leicester, Wrexham and then a loan stint with Charlton last season.

Coady, who captained Wolves and made 317 appearances for the Molineux club, admitted in an emotional post on Instagram it was a tough decision, but which came at the right time.

Coady won 10 senior caps for England and went to the Euro 2020 and World Cup 2022 finals.

The 30-year-old midfielder, who has previously played for Hibs among others, was a free agent after leaving Charlton in the summer and has been training with Preston for the past two weeks, playing for their under-21s in a 1-0 Premier League Cup win over Fleetwood.

Preston boss Graham Alexander, seeking to guide North End off the foot of the table, said: “We’re really pleased to bring Greg in. He’s been a captain, won promotions and knows the game inside out.

“Greg’s vocal, fit and motivated, which are all the key things you look for in a professional footballer. He has settled into the group really quickly and has a real willingness to show what he can do.”

« C'était pareil avec Vettel et Sainz » : Toujours en colère, Hamilton règle ses comptes avec Leclerc chez Ferrari

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

À São Paulo avant le Grand Prix du Brésil, Lewis Hamilton a haussé le ton contre Charles Leclerc. Toujours marqué par le scénario du Grand Prix du Mexique, le pilote britannique a pointé du doigt l'agressivité de son équipier chez Ferrari, coupable selon lui d'en faire bien trop contre sa propre monoplace.

Dimanche 1er novembre 2026, la Scuderia Ferrari disposait d'une occasion en or sur l'Autódromo Hermanos Rodríguez. Parti en pole position aux côtés de Charles Leclerc, qualifié au troisième rang, Lewis Hamilton espérait concrétiser la domination des monoplaces italiennes. Mais dès les premiers virages de Mexico, les deux partenaires se sont livrés une passe d'armes acharnée roue contre roue. En virant trop large, les deux monoplaces ont quitté brièvement la piste, manquant de peu l'accrochage. Sanctionné d'une pénalité de cinq secondes pour avoir quitté la piste et conservé un avantage durable lors de son dépassement, Charles Leclerc a vu Max Verstappen (Red Bull) s'intercaler et rafler la victoire. Lewis Hamilton a terminé deuxième et Charles Leclerc quatrième. Un immense gâchis.

Cette passe d'armes a provoqué la fureur immédiate de Frédéric Vasseur. Le directeur de la Scuderia Ferrari a fustigé la manœuvre au micro de Canal+ : « Ce n'est pas le résultat qu'on espère, surtout quand on est premier et deuxième après le premier virage. À Mexico, il n'y a pas eu un dépassement depuis 72 ans donc on n'espère pas finir deuxième et quatrième... J'ai du mal à l'avaler car je pense que c'est un manque de respect pour la marque, pour tous les gens qui travaillent, les efforts qu'on fait. » Le dirigeant n'a pas caché sa colère pour les 2 000 salariés de l'écurie après la perte de cette victoire promise. Charles Leclerc a d'ailleurs reconnu son erreur au micro de Canal+ : « La pénalité est clairement méritée. On ne fait tous les deux pas le virage, on freine tous les deux trop tard. Mon objectif était de redonner la position à Lewis mais Max (Verstappen) s'est intercalé entre nous donc ça devenait difficile. On va en discuter. »

Présent devant les journalistes à São Paulo, Lewis Hamilton n'a pas éludé le sujet. Le pilote britannique a accusé publiquement Charles Leclerc d'afficher une agressivité ciblée contre lui en piste, bien supérieure à celle réservée à leurs adversaires de Red Bull, McLaren ou Mercedes. Interrogé sur le comportement du Monégasque, Lewis Hamilton a dressé une comparaison sans détour avec les saisons passées de la Scuderia : « Je vois la même chose que vous. À Singapour, il était parfaitement [à la hauteur de Verstappen], roue contre roue, totalement à l'intérieur pour la prise de tête au virage 1 ; et je pense que c'était vraiment une situation différente. Si l'on regarde ses coéquipiers, c'était pareil avec Sebastian (Vettel) et avec Carlos (Sainz) », a lâché Lewis Hamilton.

Le Britannique a affirmé être toujours frustré et agacé par la manœuvre de son voisin de garage, alors que l'écurie italienne avait les armes pour s'imposer en Amérique centrale. Il a ainsi prévenu qu'une explication franche attendait son équipier : « J'étais évidemment frustré et je le suis toujours. On doit avoir une discussion et on l'aura en temps voulu à notre retour en Europe. Nous avions l'occasion de gagner pour l'équipe qui a fait un boulot incroyable en nous tirant vers le haut cette année. »

Toutefois, les deux hommes n'ont pas encore pu échanger de vive voix au Brésil. Charles Leclerc était en effet absent de la journée médias à São Paulo le jeudi 5 novembre 2026 en raison de la naissance de sa fille. L'ambiance s'annonce tendue dans le garage transalpin alors que Ferrari aborde la dernière ligne droite de la saison. Au classement des constructeurs, l'écurie italienne occupe la deuxième place avec 126 points de retard sur Mercedes, dont le pilote Kimi Antonelli a été couronné champion du monde des pilotes au Mexique. Au classement des pilotes, Lewis Hamilton figure au troisième rang, deux points derrière Max Verstappen, tandis que Charles Leclerc est cinquième.

Au-delà des rancœurs nées à Mexico, la Scuderia doit impérativement se remobiliser pour sécuriser ses positions au championnat. La prochaine course est le Grand Prix de São Paulo sur le circuit d'Interlagos, dont le départ est prévu le dimanche 8 novembre 2026 à 18h00 heure française (14h00 heure locale).

Hera spacecraft photographs asteroid from 37 miles away

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 3:19 PM EST

In September 2022, NASA’s Double Asteroid Redirection Test (DART) spacecraft smacked into a cosmic rock named Dimorphos at a speed of around 14,000 miles per hour. But mission engineers weren’t crestfallen by the news—they were thrilled. That’s because DART was specifically designed for this head-on asteroid collision. Since then, researchers around the world have poured over data from the multimillion dollar, car-sized craft’s smashing success to find ways of improving planetary defenses in the event that Earth ever finds itself in the crosshairs of a rock like Dimorphos.

In the meantime, the European Space Agency (ESA) sent its own observer, Hera, to examine the collision scene. On November 5, the ESA released the first image taken by the spacecraft. It was about 37 miles from Dimorphos’ much larger binary asteroid, Didymos. Although DART’s crash site is hidden in the shadow of the roughly 2,559-foot-wide rock, the perspective is allowing astronomers to begin examining how debris from a direct strike affects not only the primary asteroid, but any nearby objects in its vicinity.

“The first thing we said was, ‘Wow!’” Hera principal investigator Patrick Michel said in a statement. “This is the other side of Didymos from the one that DART’s camera showed us shortly before impact, so this feels like looking at a whole new world.”

Although Didymos exhibits the same central equatorial ridge visible from DART’s earlier views, the surface initially appears to be extremely different from its moonlet asteroid, Dimorphos. Because barely half a mile separates the two objects, astronomers are curious to see how much of Didymos’ features come from debris impacts.

“We have exciting work ahead of us to sort it out,” added mission scientist Michael Kueppers. “We look forward to seeing much more of both bodies and systemically mapping all the changes we identify.”

The ESA plans to release even more detailed photos of both asteroids on November 25.

Make Your Grilled Cheese Swiss-Style For Next Level Melting

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Just about any cheese is a solid choice for a grilled cheese sandwich, but ones that melt into creamy, silky stickiness are always the best when you want a great texture. That's what makes raclette one of the tastiest underrated cheeses, since its entire purpose is to melt into a gooey delight that works on just about everything.

Raclette is a high-fat, relatively high-moisture cheese from Switzerland that's particularly well-suited for melting. Traditionally, the cut sides of cheese wheels are heated near a flame or other heat source, with the gooey result scraped over a variety of vegetables and cured meats. But even in a pan and insulated by bread, it achieves the perfect, velvety texture most people look for in a grilled cheese sandwich. This is especially true if you grate it first, a simple trick for a meltier grilled cheese that also covers the bread far more evenly. Even if some drips onto the pan, it's capable of toasting into caramelized perfection rather than just quickly burning.

If you want to try it out in your next sandwich, consider seeking out traditional Swiss raclette. While some dairies outside Switzerland can recreate the moisture and fat content that make raclette melt well, other factors, including acidity, can influence its texture and melting properties. While it's not the most important factor in determining meltability, it's an often slept-on variable that can vary depending on the cheesemaking process.

A solid raclette cheese isn't flavorless, but it typically tastes quite simple. It's a "cheese's cheese," focusing on creaminess, dairy fat, and a bit of aging to create something that can be quite versatile. If you want to upgrade your grilled cheese even further, take some inspiration from classic raclette dinner ingredients, like cured meats and vegetables.

As a country with strong German cultural influences, Switzerland has a long history with pickles. The tangy vegetable is perfect on grilled cheese, especially sandwiches made with a decadently high-fat dairy product like raclette. The acidity is crucial to cutting through the heaviness for a brighter flavor, and you can use just about any type of pickle, from okra to cucumbers to cauliflower. Still, if you don't want to bulk up your meal too much, a smear of strong, spicy Swiss mustard can achieve the same effect while adding a bit of kick.

If you want to change up the texture of your grilled cheese, you can add some crunch with a thin layer of rösti, a sort of Swiss potato pancake/hash brown hybrid. Raclette is also a standard accompaniment to this potato dish, so the two go together quite well. Switzerland has quite a bit of German, French, and Italian heritage, so there's no shortage of cured meats to pair with raclette. Pork products like coppa, prosciutto, and Landjäger are especially good options, adding plenty of salt to bring out the flavors of your other ingredients.

MakeMyTrip Buys Into Daily Commutes With Quick Ride Stake

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As its air business softens, MakeMyTrip is pushing harder into everything else. Quick Ride gives it a product people use on a random Thursday, not just before a holiday. The test is whether those commuters become MakeMyTrip travelers.

MakeMyTrip Friday announced the acquisition of a majority stake in Bengaluru-based ride-pooling platform Quick Ride, expanding its ground transportation business.

The online travel company said the acquisition gives it an entry into the shared mobility segment, complementing its existing ground transport offerings, which include bus bookings through redBus and intercity car rentals through Savaari. MakeMyTrip did not say how much it invested in Quick Ride, or its precise ownership, beyond now holding a majority share.

Quick Ride connects commuters traveling along similar routes, allowing them to share rides and split commuting costs. The platform also works with corporates, IT parks and business campuses, giving it access to a customer base beyond individual travelers.

MakeMyTrip has acquired a majority stake in Bengaluru-based ride-pooling platform Quick Ride, adding daily urban commuting to a ground transport business that already includes redBus for buses and Savaari for intercity car rentals. The company did not disclose the price or its precise ownership level. Quick Ride moved its ride-pooling business and brand into a new entity, Co Ride Mobility, which MakeMyTrip now controls, while the electric vehicle taxi business stays with iDisha Info Labs and Quick Ride's existing promoters continue to run the business. The deal continues a run of acquisitions — Savaari in 2023, corporate expense platform Happay in 2024, Flamingo Transworld in group holidays, and a minority stake in visa processor Atlys — as MakeMyTrip pushes beyond flights and hotels ahead of a reported India IPO. The timing tracks with its results: non-air businesses reached 63.2% of gross bookings in the fiscal second quarter ended September 30, 2026, up from 61.5% a year earlier, and the "other" segment that includes cabs, insurance, foreign exchange, and activities posted adjusted margins of $24.4 million, up about 30% year over year. Ride-pooling gives MakeMyTrip something its other products do not: a reason for customers to open the app on days they are not traveling.

Good Riddance: The Worst Airport Lounge In The United States Closes

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Swissport Lounge at Chicago O’Hare Airport (ORD) Terminal 5 has just quietly closed. I’m about as sad as I’ll be on the day when Delta retires its latest Boeing 767-300ER…

Historically, Chicago O’Hare Terminal 5 has been a pretty lousy facility, though it has started to get better in recent years, thanks to an expansion to the terminal, and also thanks to the opening of new lounges.

Arguably the single most disappointing thing about Terminal 5 was the Swissport Lounge, which I’ve labeled the worst lounge in the United States — clearly many people agreed with me, since my review of the lounge had over 200 comments. It’s just truly one of those facilities that makes you wonder why we even bother using lounges.

The lounge had no windows, the most random collection of furniture, and a very limited selection of drinks and snacks. Unless you wanted to just guzzle alcohol, you’d be better off sitting in the terminal, in my opinion. Unfortunately for all too long, many premium airlines sent their customers here, given the lack of other lounge options. It’s quite underwhelming to be flying business class on a carrier like Etihad Airways or Qatar Airways, and then be sent here.

That brings us to the interesting update — the Swissport Lounge Chicago has closed as of October 31, 2026, so passengers are no longer being subjected to this facility.

It remains to be seen what happens with this lounge space, as that hasn’t been announced one way or another. In April 2025, Swissport’s head of North America lounges said that the company had “submitted a proposal to the Chicago Department of Aviation (CDA) for a multi-million-dollar renovation and expansion of our current Terminal 5 lounge at O’Hare International Airport.” However, I haven’t been able to find any updates on that, so I’m not sure what the latest is there.

It’s worth acknowledging just how much better the lounge situation at Chicago O’Hare Terminal 5 has gotten in recent years:

If we get another lounge at Terminal 5, that would of course be awesome, so I’m curious to see how this evolves. Chicago O’Hare is the biggest airport in the country to not have any sort of a credit card branded lounge, so I hope that eventually changes. The issue is the extent to which the terminals are dominated by their primary tenants (American and United), combined with the lack of airside connections to Terminal 5.

The Swissport Lounge Chicago O’Hare Terminal 5 has suddenly closed. I’m not sad to see the United States’ worst airport lounge close, though I am curious to see what is next for this space. Fortunately the Terminal 5 lounge situation has improved nicely in recent years, and there are now several solid new options. I do wish we’d still see a proper credit card lounge, though.

What do you make of the Swissport Lounge Chicago closing? Are you happy or elated?

Marianne Moore on the Three Elements of Persuasive Writing

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

Several years ago, rummaging through the archives of the Academy of American Poets, I came upon a box labeled “Ballots 1950” — the record of the secret vote by the chancellors the year the Academy’s prestigious fellowship was awarded to E.E. Cummings, catapulting him into renown. The voting process is a black box — no one outside the Academy ever finds out who else is in the running and by how much the winner wins.

Leafing through the ballots, one other name appeared over and over, so much so that I was impelled to count.

Marianne Moore had lost by one vote, never knowing how close she had come. It would be many more years until, at 77, she was finally awarded the fellowship.

Long before that, before she won the Pulitzer Prize and the National Book Award (sharing a table with Rachel Carson at the ceremony), Moore had set down her views on writing in a series of essays later collected in the out-of-print gem Predilections (public library). Pulsating through them is a reckoning with the impossible task of the writer — to weave tapestries of truth and meaning from the tenuous thread of words on the ramshackle loom of language.

In an essay titled “Feeling and Precision,” Moore writes:

Feeling at its deepest — as we all have reason to know — tends to be inarticulable. If it does manage to be articulate, it is likely to seem overcondensed, so that the author is resisted as being enigmatic or disobliging or arrogant.

How we name what we feel is not so much a matter of our writing style as of our style of being, because in order to articulate something we must first apprehend it and we apprehend every smallest thing with the whole person — with the frame of reference that is our entire life, the sum of our experience and memory. When “one of New York’s more painstaking magazines” asked Moore to distill her poetic style into a formula, she fought back the “dictatorial” reflex to quip:

You don’t devise a rhythm, the rhythm is the person, and the sentence but a radiograph of the personality.

And yet a personality can write with more or less persuasion — that is, write more or less well — depending on what the person brings to the writing. In another essay from the collection, Moore identifies the three psychological elements necessary for persuasive writing: “humility, concentration, and gusto.” A generation after Mark Twain assured his friend Helen Keller when she was accused of plagiarism that “substantially all ideas are second-hand, consciously and unconsciously drawn from a million outside sources,” she writes:

Humility… is armor, for it realizes that it is impossible to be original, in the sense of doing something that has never been thought of before. Originality is in any case a by-product of sincerity; that is to say, of feeling that is honest and accordingly rejects anything that might cloud the impression.

By “concentration” Moore means a kind of discipline — annealing the essence of the sentiment by cutting away all superfluous explanations, elaborations, and distractions of stylistic posturing, being maximally truthfully in the most minimal way possible. Observing that there is always a “helpless sincerity which precipitates a poem” and that a good poem is always “a concentrate,” she writes:

Concentration — indispensable to persuasion — may feel to itself crystal clear, yet be through its very compression the opposite… I myself would rather be told too little than too much.

Long before we had the language of extrinsic and intrinsic motivation, of finite and infinite games, Moore uses a lovely word, now dusty, for that peculiar private zeal propelling all creative work with its twin dynamos of discipline and deliverance: “gusto.” Echoing Rachel Carson’s abiding advice on writing — “If you write what you yourself sincerely think and feel and are interested in,” she had counseled a young writer, “the chances are very high that you will interest other people as well.” — Moore offers:

Gusto thrives on freedom, and freedom in art, as in life, is the result of a discipline imposed by ourselves. Moreover, any writer overwhelmingly honest about pleasing himself is almost sure to please others.

She maps the fundamental relationship between the three:

Humility is an indispensable teacher, enabling concentration to heighten gusto.

When creating in integrity with these three values, it ceases to matter how the work is received because the process of locating and articulating the truth as you feel it, the world as you see it, is its own reward. In what may be the best advice I have encountered on how to orient to your own work, Moore writes:

There are always objecters, but we must not be sensitive about not being liked or not being printed… The thing is to see the vision and not deny it; or care and admit that we do.

Complement with Walt Whitman on how to keep criticism from sinking your soul and Mary Oliver’s advice on writing, then savor the moving story of how Marianne Moore saved a rare tree with a poem.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

Do You Speak Geek? 5 Tech Terms That Endure, and 5 That Got Defragged

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Level up your vocab with these tech phrases that'll give you major rizz.

Real talk: I’m not that up to speed on today’s tech vocabulary. That realization clocked me over the head this week when the Scotland-based Collins dictionary came out with its list of new and notable words for 2026. The words include p(doom), which refers to the probability of AI-caused doom; mogging, meaning to outshine or upstage someone; friendslop, a type of low-budget video game played among friends; and ghost jobs, jobs advertised with no real position or intent to hire someone.

Vibe-coding, which was Collins’ Word of the Year for 2025, refers to a conversational way to teach an AI model without typing in all that pesky code. Enshittification, which the American Dialect Society chose as its 2023 Word of the Year, means the gradual, profit-driven decline in the quality of, well, I guess, everything.

Maybe you know and use these terms regularly; maybe you don’t. But, in an effort to stop myself from becoming that Steve Buscemi faking-it-in-high-school meme, I wanted to lay out some tech terms that have a permanent home in the popular lexicon and the words, phrases and abbreviations that haven’t quite stood the test of time. (Note to self: Stop saying “emoticon.”)

Selfie: You know what a selfie is; my mother, on the other hand, may not. The word for taking a photo of yourself with any camera-enabled device was first coined in 2002 on an Australian internet chat forum, of all places, but didn’t become mainstream until 2013.

Content creator: “Influencer” also works here, to refer to a person who shares information on a tech platform and builds an audience of online followers. A related word, “parasocial,” refers to the one-sided relationship listeners or viewers may have with the creator or influencer, who, in contrast, doesn’t know they exist.

Podcast: The Oxford Dictionary named podcast the Word of the Year back in 2005. Since then, the term, which is a mashup of iPod and broadcast and refers to a digital program made available on-demand for streaming or download, has remained relevant. The iPod, however? Not so much. It was discontinued in 2022.

WFH (work from home): The trend of working from your own home via computer saw a substantial uptick during and after the 2020 COVID-19 pandemic. According to a 2022 survey by the US Bureau of Labor Statistics, 23% of Americans work remotely.

Unfriend: The New Oxford American Dictionary‘s 2009 Word of the Year became popularized thanks to Facebook, but the term that means ending a friendship has actually been around for quite some time. NPR called it “the Sleeping Beauty of words,” as the term dates back to the 17th century, when it basically meant the same thing.

Metaverse: Remember when Mark Zuckerberg poured like $80 billion into a virtual reality project that was being touted as the internet platform of the future? The metaverse and its flagship app, Horizon Worlds, were announced in 2021. Five years later, it all fell apart. Now, Zuck is investing a ton in AI, which he’s touting as the internet technology of the future. Second verse, same as the first?

NFT: Just a couple of years ago, you couldn’t go online without reading about NFTs. Nonfungible tokens are digital identifiers to certify ownership of, well, a digital thing of value. Heck, remember when President Donald Trump released an NFT? After the boom in popularity, and the subsequent crash in value of most of them, the term NFT has all but left the public consciousness.

World Wide Web: It’s the internet, folks. Or, the information superhighway if you’re nasty.

Defrag: Back in my day, when we needed better performance from our Windows-based computers, we’d defragment the hard drive. There were plenty of tools you could use to do so. Apparently, this is no longer a thing people do, which is fun, since I still like to use the term in everyday situations. While we’re at it, I’ll be right back. I need to go defrag the car.

Emoticon: Technically, emoticons are made from standard keyboard characters read sideways, such as 🙂 to generate a smiley face. Emoji are graphical images that you can select in various communication programs. (Sometimes, if you try to type an old-school emoticon, the program you’re working in will just grab it and turn it into an emoji.) These days, we just say emoji, even if the word sounds more like a dessert.

CNET People’s Picks: Readers Say This Brand Wins as the Top Laptop Choice

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

If you prefer a PC, there are still some non-Apple brands worth considering.

Shopping for a new laptop for the holidays, but not sure which brand to choose from? Our readers do.

We recently surveyed CNET readers as part of our People’s Picks awards to find out what they think about their laptop brand to help you make a more sound choice for your next laptop. We listed several well-known names, including Dell and Lenovo. We asked about everything that matters most on a daily basis, like battery life, durability and even its performance for handling tasks like gaming and graphics. Out of 10 categories, one brand wins most of them: Apple.

Apple swept up eight of the awards for its signature MacBooks and claimed the overall winner for CNET’s People’s Picks award. Here’s why.

Apple wins the overall award in CNET’s People’s Picks, and also takes home the overall satisfaction rating, with 74.2% of Apple users saying they’re extremely satisfied with their laptop. In comparison, 22.5% say they’re satisfied — bringing Apple to a near-perfect overall satisfaction score of 96.7%.

Apple’s overall impression also speaks to other awards it wins from CNET readers, including high ratings for its impressive battery life, display quality and high-quality build. All of those categories and more sum up Apple’s brand loyalty nod, with 68% of MacBook owners saying they would buy the same laptop and model from Apple again.

How much are people paying for MacBooks? CNET found that 43.5% of Apple users paid at least $1,500. Until earlier in 2026, the MacBook Air was Apple’s entry-level laptop, priced at least $1,299. The introduction of the budget-friendly MacBook Neo brought that down to a more reasonable $699. However, despite Apple’s laptops being more expensive than those from other brands, they still ranked high for their value proposition. Part of the reason for that is that MacBooks received high marks for hardware reliability. “Apple’s MacBooks might cost more than the average laptop, but their premium build quality means they’re built for the long haul,” said Matt Elliott, CNET’s laptop editor.

If you plan to have your laptop for a while, you may be impressed by other features that make work easier and faster, like its M-series central processing unit for task management and AI capabilities, great battery life and a one-of-a-kind operating system — currently MacOS 27. All of the features and specifications bundled together make Apple’s perception as a high-end laptop worth the investment for long-term satisfaction.

Apple clinches a near-perfect score for its value perception. According to our survey results, 44% of Apple users said their MacBook has exceptional value. Others say their MacBook is worth what they paid for it (50.9%) — for a total positive score of 94.6%.

Digging deeper into Apple’s cost-to-value rating, 81.8% of Apple owners paid at least $1,000 for their laptops, which may seem costly, but Apple users seem OK with the price. That could be because, even though Apple has only three laptop models in a handful of sizes in its lineup, each one delivers solid performance and storage.

Take Apple’s proprietary processing chips, for example — each upgrade since the M1 release has improved the laptop’s speed, battery life and performance. Now the M6 can handle the MacBook’s AI capabilities, storage and high-resolution graphics. And for all of that power, most MacBooks are relatively thin and lightweight, making them easier for mobility — just a few of the reasons Apple users may be OK with paying more for what they’re getting.

Battery life is a big deal for many laptop buyers, and that’s another area where Apple delivers. Over a quarter of those surveyed (25.7%) said their MacBook’s battery life exceeds 10 hours — which is more than enough for a full day of work in most cases. And 18.7% say their MacBook’s battery lasts between 8 and 10 hours — totaling 44.4% of MacBook users saying their battery lasts 8 hours or more.

Comparatively, non-Apple laptop brands fall short in this category. Only 16.4% of Lenovo users say their laptop’s battery lasts at least 8 hours, and even fewer for Dell (12.7%) and HP (11.8%). If you’re leaning toward a MacBook for its battery life, CNET’s lab test shows that the MacBook Neo lasts 13 hours and 26 minutes for online streaming, whereas the Apple MacBook Air 13-inch M5 (2026 model) lasts 17 hours and 2 minutes.

Apple sweeps the brightness, color accuracy and clarity category with 99.2% of its users either satisfied or extremely satisfied with their laptop’s screen performance. No Apple users reported dissatisfaction.

“Apple has long been a favorite for creative professionals and content creators for good reason: Its MacBook displays are some of the best you’ll find on any laptop,” said Josh Goldman, CNET’s managing editor for computers. “While Apple may use marketing names like Retina and Liquid Retina to describe display features, they do translate into sharp and clear text and images, with excellent brightness, contrast, color gamut coverage and color accuracy.”

The new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR even goes beyond what MacBook users have come to expect. It’s Apple’s first OLED touchscreen laptop and features next-level brightness, color and contrast.

Apple receives high ratings: 80% of MacBook owners say it has premium build quality, 18.2% say it’s solid, and those results don’t surprise us.

And MacBooks seem durable and reliable, with 87% of Apple users reporting no hardware failures on their MacBooks, while 8% say minor software fixes resolved their issues. Only 3% needed minor hardware repairs, and 2.2% needed major repairs — which could be fixes such as a battery or screen replacement.

Our editors say the M5 MacBook Air is thin and lightweight, but still has a well-built aluminum design. And the M5 Pro MacBook Pro is sturdy, too. What sets Apple’s laptop hardware apart could be its aluminum chassis — the casing that holds all of the components together.

“Apple makes three lines of MacBooks — the Neo, Air and Pro — and focuses on making those the best they can possibly be. They are all built to deliver a premium experience using high-quality materials, and that even includes the entry-level MacBook Neo,” said Goldman.

Lastly, Apple uses durable materials, such as the 100% recycled aluminum alloy it says encloses the 14-inch MacBook Pro. And the tech giant has several testing facilities to test MacBook durability.

When it comes to getting your daily tasks done, most CNET readers with MacBooks (70.9%) say Apple’s laptops handle workloads flawlessly. It’s worth noting that 45% use their MacBook for work tasks, but no Apple users reported poor performance.

Here’s why that might be: Apple’s laptops are packed with top-notch performance specs and features, like their powerful processors, unified memory and fast storage. Better processor performance means enhanced graphics, on-device AI capabilities and faster task speeds, all of which add up to being able to finish work, play games or stream videos without issues or lags caused by your laptop’s hardware.

On the software side, MacOS 27 Golden Gate gives you built-in AI features, like Siri AI and Visual Intelligence. And you can organize your Safari tabs by topic and use the Notify Me tool to be notified of website changes, like price drops or breaking news. Plus, you can create shortcuts to automate tasks. Coupling all of these features and specifications explains why Apple users rate MacBooks so highly for getting the job done.

Apple leads the way on brand retention with 68% of Apple users planning to buy the same MacBook model again, while 29.1% would buy another model from Apple’s lineup. Only 3% would switch to another brand that’s not Apple.

Usually, when you buy one Apple product, you’re likely to stick to the brand’s ecosystem, especially since Apple has its own operating systems to access its unique features and work seamlessly with other Apple products with Apple’s Continuity feature. For example, you can start an email on your iPhone, but finish typing it on your MacBook. Or you can call or send a text from your laptop without reaching for your iPhone. Other features work across Apple devices, including iCloud, Universal Clipboard and AirDrop.

Asus wins as a midtier laptop choice with 67% of Asus owners paying between $500 and $1,499. There’s a sweet spot in this range with 38% spending between $500 and $999, and 28% paying between $1,000 and $1,499.

If you’re planning to spend that amount on a new laptop, you may also be pleased to know that Asus earns a higher satisfaction rating compared with other non-Apple laptop brands — 48% of Asus users say they’re extremely satisfied.

Asus laptops have a few features and specifications that may speak to that rating, regardless of budget. For example, the Zenbook A14 has a thin, lightweight design that’s rigid, with long battery life and an OLED display with strong contrast and color performance. This Copilot Plus PC sells for $1,600 (but retailers like Best Buy regularly have it on sale for less). Comparatively, these premium features from other brands could cost more or sacrifice important must-have features for the price.

Asus has other laptops worth exploring depending on your needs, like the budget-friendly Asus ZenBook S 14 or the premium Asus ROG Zephyrus G14 that could be good for gamers and creators.

Over one-third of Acer laptop owners (38%) paid less than $500 for their laptop — which is greater than other laptop brands in this price range. Yet, 44% of Acer users say their laptop has exceptional value, while 50% say the value is fair — bringing a total positive cost-to-value score of over 94%. Acer also has an overall satisfaction score of 92.6% — the highest of non-Apple laptop brands.

If you’re impressed by Acer’s value and satisfaction scores, there are a few budget-friendly Acer options to consider. The Acer Aspire 16 AI wins CNET’s Editors’ Choice award. It has a big 16-inch screen but is still lightweight (3.5 pounds). And it has good battery life (over 21 hours on CNET’s battery drain test). There are a few other pluses, like the 1440p web camera that may be good for video conferencing and a sizable touchpad. All of those features and more add up to $700, which could make it a good computer for work or casual browsing. If you’re looking to spend slightly less, its predecessor, the Aspire 14 AI, is originally $639, but you may be able to score deals on both models during holiday sales.

Even though Apple is highly recommended by CNET readers, there are plenty of other laptop models and brands worth considering depending on your needs. Here are a few non-Apple laptop brands that are runner-ups to CNET’s People’s Picks winners that you shouldn’t count out of your laptop search.

Lenovo is best known for packing a powerful laptop into a compact design with tablet functionality, like its Yoga line, and its durable ThinkPad business models. But CNET readers with Lenovo laptops say the brand’s battery life stands out most compared with other non-MacBook laptops.

Survey findings show that 16% of Lenovo owners say their laptops can run for 8 hours or more. And 10.5% say their laptop battery lasts between 8 and 10 hours, while 6% say their Lenovo laptops can withstand at least 10 hours before needing a charge. That’s more than what readers say about other non-Apple brands. The Yoga 9i 2-in-1 Aura Edition lasts 25 hours and 45 minutes — making it one of the best laptop batteries CNET experts have tested.

Goldman said Lenovo has such a large and varied PC selection using the latest chips from Intel, AMD, Qualcomm and Nvidia that there is something for everyone and every budget. Even those who spend less on a Lenovo laptop can expect solid performance, including battery life.

If your laptop’s hardware fails or it breaks, you may be in the market for a new laptop sooner than expected. Think about cooling fan problems, overheating or your laptop not powering on at all. Over half (68%) of Lenovo laptop owners said they haven’t had any hardware issues with their laptops — the most compared to other non-MacBooks.

Beyond hardware durability, Lenovo also scores highly for its build and construction — overall making it a sturdy laptop choice. In our survey, 38% of Lenovo laptop owners say their laptop has a premium feel, while 51% say it’s solid — totaling a positive score of 89%.

If you’re looking for recommendations, the Lenovo Yoga Slim 7x Gen 11 has an all-metal design. And the Lenovo ThinkPad X1 Carbon Gen 14 is a business laptop with Lenovo’s well-known X1 Carbon exterior and new Space Frame design, which Elliott says makes it easier to repair or replace parts if needed.

Many Lenovo laptop users are loyal to the brand, which speaks to the overall positive satisfaction rating of 83%. If Lenovo owners were in the market for a new laptop, nearly three in four (73.2%) say they would buy another Lenovo laptop. Breaking down the percentage, 33.6% say they would buy the same model again. Meanwhile, 39.6% would buy a different model but stick to Lenovo.

Here’s why that might be. Lenovo offers a plethora of options to choose from based on your needs — while still evolving its laptops. For instance, the Lenovo Legion 5i Gen 10 has an open M.2 slot for a second storage drive and an OLED screen for crisp, colorful images — making it perfect for gaming. Its Yoga two-in-ones let you seamlessly switch between a tablet and traditional laptop, while Lenovo’s ThinkPad and ThinkBook business laptops offer everything from ultralight 2-pound bodies to expanding rollable screens. And its new designs don’t sacrifice the must-have needs like long battery life, durability and chip efficiency that lend to strong performance.

Asus wins over nearly half of its laptop owners, with 47.5% giving it an exceptional value rating  — exceeding expectations for its price. Take our Editors’ Choice pick, the Asus Zenbook A14. It’s super thin and lightweight, but it’s still sturdy thanks to its Ceralumnium body. Plus, it has supreme 24-hour battery life, 1TB of storage and an OLED display — all for $1,200. While that’s more expensive than some laptops, it’s what’s included in that price that makes it worthwhile compared with other non-Apple laptop brands.

Asus is the top non-Apple laptop option to consider for tasks, with 47.7% of CNET readers giving it high ratings. That’s followed by 40.4% of Asus laptop owners who say it has very good task performance. When it comes to tasks, 37% primarily use their Asus laptops for work, while 5% use theirs for gaming.

Asus is well known for having lightweight laptops with all-day-and-night battery life and stunning display quality. Asus laptops like the Asus ROG Zephyrus G16 have a good port selection, which could be good for gaming, work or simply connecting different devices without the need for additional adapters. And the Asus Zenbook S 14 has a strong neural processing unit that contributes to its high-speed AI performance.

Asus scores highest for its screen brightness and clarity, with over half (51%) extremely satisfied with its screen quality and brightness, while 38.6% say they’re satisfied, giving Asus an overall satisfaction rate of 90%. One common thread we noticed in Asus laptops is their OLED screens, which deliver high-resolution video and graphics. Asus laptops also combine stunning display quality with excellent performance and at surprisingly low prices for all of their features.

For example, the Asus Zenbook 14 isn’t the highest-quality OLED we’ve seen, but it still produces clear text and graphics on a 14-inch screen, along with exceptional black levels, Elliott said. That’s still better than LCDs that many other non-MacBooks have, and could be the reason Asus owners rate it so highly in this category.

Dell wins our readers over as a top choice for high-end laptops. One-third (33.3%) of Dell owners spent over $1,500, and of that percentage, 15.2% spent over $2,000.

Dell’s premium laptops largely cater to business and gaming with their standout features and performance. The Alienware 16X Aurora is Dell’s midrange gaming laptop with user-replaceable RAM, a bright display and strong performance for $2,280. But if you want more gaming features, Dell has the Alienware 16 Area-51, which has even better gaming performance, upgradable RAM and three SSD slots for $2,530. And if you’re looking for a non-Mac alternative that comes close to MacBook quality and performance, the higher-end Dell XPS 14 is the one to choose. It has the Intel Core Ultra X7 358H CPU, Intel Arc B390 graphics and 1TB of storage for $1,950.

CNET commissioned Alchemer to conduct the People’s Picks survey to compile data. The survey was carried out online, and all figures are from Alchemer. The total sample comprised 1,003 adults. The figures have been weighted and are representative of all US adults (aged 18 plus). Fieldwork was undertaken from Sept. 1 to Oct. 10, 2026.

Midterm elections will put AI and data centers on trial, but Big Tech may end up winning anyway

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

THIS WEEK IN ENTERPRISE by Robert Hof

It may be a little soon to determine the ultimate winners and losers among enterprise suppliers after the midterm elections, but the early line is: Big Tech wins and data center suppliers operators such as chipmakers and neoclouds lose. Or will everybody lose?

The day after the elections, stocks of many tech companies shot up, a bit paradoxically because investors apparently believe potential limits on AI and data centers could tamp down spending on compute and thus improve tech company margins that have made investors nervous. But then on Thursday, tech stocks almost across the board plunged back down even more for a collective loss of $2.5 trillion in market cap — the Nasdaq fell more than 5.5% — on more generalized worries that, well, the AI boom could slow. Suffice to say, investors are still sorting things out.

Marvell Semiconductor this week made a surprisingly huge acquisition with Lumentum, a bet on the AI boom continuing. That is, if it comes off: The drop in Lumentum’s stock price has some speculating it could hunt around for another offer.

And the AI juggernaut indeed continues apace, judging from strong tech earnings, though even pre-election, they reflected investor wariness. AMD, Supermicro and Arista all beat easily, as well as Palantir and Kyndryl, whose stock rocketed 30%. But chipmakers Qualcomm, Arm and Globalfoundries didn’t get the love despite good quarters — especially in Thursday’s bloodbath.

All of which raises the question that Dave Vellante posed in his Breaking Analysis last weekend: Can enterprise AI pay for the boom? Dave and analyst Crawford Del Prete discussed the prospects.

Next week’s earnings reports include Cisco, CoreWeave, Applied Materials, Cerebras and Nebius, any or all of which could move markets.

Here’s all of this week’s enterprise and emerging tech news, analysis and commentary from SiliconANGLE and beyond:

Amazon, Microsoft and other tech giants close higher after Democrats’ midterm sweep No, data centers won’t get shut down, and AI will continue apace — just regulated as it needs to be, and we can hope it’s in the right way to protect people and communities and innovation all at the same time. We can walk and chew gum at the same time if we put our mind to it. Hard to imagine the Dems could do worse than Trump and the GOP have been doing for years now.

OpenAI makes new data center commitments, AI policy recommendations That was quick; we’ll see if it makes a difference.

With William Saliba now available once more, Arteta has confirmed Konsa's status ahead of action this Saturday.

Kenyan doomsday sect leader jailed for 200 years for murder of 190 children

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Paul Mackenzie convicted over deaths of children and one adult after followers were ordered to starve themselves and their families

The leader of a Kenyan doomsday sect has been sentenced to 200 years in prison for the murders of 190 children and one adult in one of the world’s worst cult disasters in recent history.

The sentence, comprising 100 years for murder by starvation and 100 years by direct homicide, came two weeks after the court in Kenya’s coastal city of Mombasa found the cult leader guilty of the crimes.

Prosecutors said Paul Mackenzie and his Good News International church ran ​a Christian doomsday sect whose members were ordered to starve themselves and their children to death to go to heaven before the world ended.

His accomplices threatened and beat victims to ensure they followed his orders. The emaciated bodies were found scattered in shallow graves and dumped in latrines. Some had been buried alive.

Mackenzie was arrested in 2023 after police received a tipoff, and weeks later authorities began uncovering bodies in shallow graves in the Shakahola forest near the coast in south-eastern Kenya.

In total, more than 400 bodies have been recovered. Many were severely decomposed and only 26 could be identified.

Mackenzie denied murder charges, saying he did not force the victims to starve, but the court rejected that argument.

The Shakahola tragedy has gripped Kenya since the discovery of mass graves in the forest more than three years ago.

It raised questions about how Mackenzie was able to continue operating despite being on the radar of the authorities since 2017, when police searched his premises.

In 2019, authorities shut down Mackenzie’s church and he relocated ‌to the Shakahola forest, police said.

The case has also led to calls for tougher oversight of churches and other religious organisations in a country where religious faith plays a prominent role in public life.

Mackenzie, a former taxi driver, forbade cult members from sending their children to school and from going to hospital when they were ill, describing such institutions as satanic.

Cult members lived in makeshift homes of polythene sheeting and thatch that Mackenzie divided into areas with biblical names such as Jerusalem and Judea.

The plight of his followers began ​to emerge in March 2023, when a man told ​police his brother and wife had starved their children ​to death in the forest on Mackenzie’s orders.

Mackenzie was arrested but released on bail, after which relatives said he brought forward the date ​on which he told his followers the ‌world would end. Police rearrested him and ​started combing through the forest more systematically and began exhuming mass graves.

Last month’s judgment covered 191 deaths while investigations into more than 200 other deaths are continuing.

Justice Diana Kavedza-Mochache said in a hearing lasting more than eight hours that in some cases children were isolated and denied food while their parents watched.

Mackenzie faces other charges over the forest deaths, including those of terrorism, which he has denied.

Prosecutors said that while he was in detention he also directed the deaths of 52 people in Kwa Binzaro village, about 18 miles from Shakahola. Mackenzie has pleaded not guilty in that case.
