Keeping the Diwali Flame Burning in New York City

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The akhand diya, the continuously burning flame, has one purpose: to stay lit. It is a small but enduring presence, tended to around the clock, its care passed from one person to the next so that the light never goes out. I’ve often thought about how much of life is like this, how we are constantly passing the torch from one version of ourselves to another.

As a teenager, I remember feeling that I had crossed an invisible threshold into womanhood. I was becoming someone different from the girl I had been, carrying parts of her with me but beginning to understand the world through a new lens. Now, years later, I find myself at another such transition: I am becoming a different version of a woman, one I am still getting to know.

In many ways, this transition is happening outside of me. Thirteen years ago, I moved away from my family in Mumbai and the home where Diwali had always been celebrated. For all those years, the rituals continued, even as I participated in them from thousands of miles away, mostly over video calls. Back in Mumbai, Diwali meant a house full of family and friends, kitchens bustling with cooking and frying, and the warm glow of diyas illuminating the mandir. I was no longer physically there, but I remained part of it. This year, for the first time, I hosted my parents for Diwali in the home I’ve built for myself in New York City.

It felt cataclysmic, or at least I was making it feel that way in my head. As I welcomed a different version of myself, I was also taking on a new role within my family. I was no longer simply the daughter returning to their house for Diwali; I was the one opening my home, preparing the food, tending to the rituals, and making space for the people who first taught me what this celebration means. There is perhaps no greater joy than watching the people I love receive and experience care. And yet, this was a responsibility I had chosen to take on, and one that felt particularly daunting as the eldest daughter. I wanted to get it right.

For me, that care has always found its way to the table. Food is how my family celebrates, how we mark occasions, and, perhaps most importantly, how we look after one another. So naturally, much of my energy went into planning the meal. Despite my best efforts, my dad promptly assigned himself the task of making the goat kari—apparently, even after 13 years away, some things hadn’t changed. We headed to our local butcher in Brooklyn to buy fresh goat meat, and almost immediately, his attention turned to the spice tolerance of my guests.

“How much spice can your friends tolerate?” he kept asking me. “Should I keep it at a level six? But our level six might be their level ten. What do I do?” he lamented. “Relax, they’ll be able to handle it! Just make it our level five,” I told him.

I gave in and handed over part of the kitchen to my mother, too. She took charge of the paneer, cooking generous chunks in a luscious onion-tomato gravy and making the chapatis. Selfishly, I didn’t mind relinquishing that responsibility: This would be my one chance for the year to eat chapatis fresh off the tava, puffed up and piping hot, and made by my mother.

As the days drew closer, I found myself loosening my grip on the plans I’d made. My parents had their own ideas about what needed to be cooked, and while I was initially resistant to them taking over (any immigrant will tell you how hard it is to get our parents to just relax!), I was learning that hosting didn’t necessarily mean doing everything myself. It also meant making room for everyone to contribute and allowing new rituals to form.

The evening began with freshly fried moong vadas. My mother, of course, insisted on frying them for everyone, right before serving. Made from soaked and ground mung lentils, these little fritters are crisp and golden on the outside, with a soft, almost creamy interior. They have a naturally earthy, nutty flavor from the lentils, sharpened with green chiles, ginger, and spices.

Moong vadas are best eaten immediately, still hot out of the oil, with a tangy green chutney. The scene reminded me of Diwalis back in Mumbai, when the vadas rarely even made it to the serving platter: Everyone would gather around the kitchen, waiting for the next batch to come out of the kadai, reaching for them before they had a chance to cool. I saw the same thing happening in my kitchen in New York.

In the meantime, my friends Leanne and Christina took charge of the tamarind margaritas, my ode to the soft, sweet-tart tamarind candies I grew up savoring as a kid. The tangy cocktails were a welcome counterpoint to the savory fritters, the perfect way to wake up our taste buds (and made exercising some restraint with those moong vadas before dinner particularly difficult). My brother helped set the table and move furniture around, just as we used to do for our parents back in Mumbai. There was something deeply comforting about watching us fall into these old rhythms.

The table eventually came together with a mix of dishes that felt both familiar and celebratory. There was chana masala from my cookbook, East to East, alongside my dad’s goat kari, slow-cooked with spices until the meat was tender. My mother’s saucy tomato-onion paneer was accompanied by a pile of chapatis that disappeared almost as quickly as they came off the stove. We served everything family-style, laying out the dishes on kansa thalis, traditional Indian plates made from bronze or bell metal, an alloy of copper and tin. They reminded me of the way my grandparents served meals, and I wanted to bring a little of that into our Diwali celebration.

At the center of the spread was a vegetable pulao, fragrant basmati rice cooked with carrots, cabbage, cauliflower, and whole spices. Alongside it, I placed bowls of pickled onions and mango achaar, whose sharp, tangy bite cuts through the richness of the karis. There was also cucumber raita, cool and creamy, offering a welcome respite from the heat of the spices. These accompaniments might seem like small additions, but they are essential to the balance of an Indian meal. Every bite can be a little different, depending on what you bring together on your plate.

For dessert, I made homemade gulab jamun, my brother’s favorite. The soft dumplings are made from milk solids and fried until golden, then soaked in a warm saffron-and-cardamom-scented sugar syrup.

As I sat cuddled among both my families, I realized that while it wasn’t the perfectly orchestrated dinner I’d imagined when I first started planning, it was something better. The family I was born into celebrating Diwali alongside the family I’d chosen and built in New York, all gathered around the same table and enjoying every minute of it. Perhaps keeping the flame alive was never about re-creating the past exactly as it was but about allowing it to illuminate whatever comes next.

Hungry for even more stories to sink your teeth into?

So naturally, much of my energy went into planning the meal. Despite my best efforts, my dad promptly assigned himself the task of making the goat kari—apparently, even after 13 years away, some things hadn’t changed. We headed to our local butcher in Brooklyn to buy fresh goat meat, and almost immediately, his attention turned to the spice tolerance of my guests.

“How much spice can your friends tolerate?” he kept asking me. “Should I keep it at a level six? But our level six might be their level ten. What do I do?” he lamented. “Relax, they’ll be able to handle it! Just make it our level five,” I told him.

I gave in and handed over part of the kitchen to my mother, too. She took charge of the paneer, cooking generous chunks in a luscious onion-tomato gravy and making the chapatis. Selfishly, I didn’t mind relinquishing that responsibility: This would be my one chance for the year to eat chapatis fresh off the tava, puffed up and piping hot, and made by my mother.

As the days drew closer, I found myself loosening my grip on the plans I’d made. My parents had their own ideas about what needed to be cooked, and while I was initially resistant to them taking over (any immigrant will tell you how hard it is to get our parents to just relax!), I was learning that hosting didn’t necessarily mean doing everything myself. It also meant making room for everyone to contribute and allowing new rituals to form.

The evening began with freshly fried moong vadas. My mother, of course, insisted on frying them for everyone, right before serving. Made from soaked and ground mung lentils, these little fritters are crisp and golden on the outside, with a soft, almost creamy interior. They have a naturally earthy, nutty flavor from the lentils, sharpened with green chiles, ginger, and spices.

Moong vadas are best eaten immediately, still hot out of the oil, with a tangy green chutney. The scene reminded me of Diwalis back in Mumbai, when the vadas rarely even made it to the serving platter: Everyone would gather around the kitchen, waiting for the next batch to come out of the kadai, reaching for them before they had a chance to cool. I saw the same thing happening in my kitchen in New York.

In the meantime, my friends Leanne and Christina took charge of the tamarind margaritas, my ode to the soft, sweet-tart tamarind candies I grew up savoring as a kid. The tangy cocktails were a welcome counterpoint to the savory fritters, the perfect way to wake up our taste buds (and made exercising some restraint with those moong vadas before dinner particularly difficult). My brother helped set the table and move furniture around, just as we used to do for our parents back in Mumbai. There was something deeply comforting about watching us fall into these old rhythms.

The table eventually came together with a mix of dishes that felt both familiar and celebratory. There was chana masala from my cookbook, East to East, alongside my dad’s goat kari, slow-cooked with spices until the meat was tender. My mother’s saucy tomato-onion paneer was accompanied by a pile of chapatis that disappeared almost as quickly as they came off the stove. We served everything family-style, laying out the dishes on kansa thalis, traditional Indian plates made from bronze or bell metal, an alloy of copper and tin. They reminded me of the way my grandparents served meals, and I wanted to bring a little of that into our Diwali celebration.

At the center of the spread was a vegetable pulao, fragrant basmati rice cooked with carrots, cabbage, cauliflower, and whole spices. Alongside it, I placed bowls of pickled onions and mango achaar, whose sharp, tangy bite cuts through the richness of the karis. There was also cucumber raita, cool and creamy, offering a welcome respite from the heat of the spices. These accompaniments might seem like small additions, but they are essential to the balance of an Indian meal. Every bite can be a little different, depending on what you bring together on your plate.

For dessert, I made homemade gulab jamun, my brother’s favorite. The soft dumplings are made from milk solids and fried until golden, then soaked in a warm saffron-and-cardamom-scented sugar syrup.

As I sat cuddled among both my families, I realized that while it wasn’t the perfectly orchestrated dinner I’d imagined when I first started planning, it was something better. The family I was born into celebrating Diwali alongside the family I’d chosen and built in New York, all gathered around the same table and enjoying every minute of it. Perhaps keeping the flame alive was never about re-creating the past exactly as it was but about allowing it to illuminate whatever comes next.

« Je ne me sens coupable de rien » : Jorge Jesus réagit à la décision de Cristiano Ronaldo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Présent ce vendredi 6 novembre 2026 en conférence de presse à la Cidade do Futebol à Oeiras pour dévoiler sa liste de 28 joueurs, Jorge Jesus a tenu à clarifier la situation autour de Cristiano Ronaldo. Le sélectionneur portugais a en effet révélé n'avoir appris la retraite internationale de son attaquant que le jeudi 5 novembre 2026, au moment de la publication de sa vidéo et de son communiqué officiel.

Ce vendredi, la Cidade do Futebol à Oeiras a été le théâtre d'une annonce particulièrement attendue. Avant que Jorge Jesus ne présente sa convocation pour les rencontres de Ligue des nations face au Danemark et au pays de Galles, la vidéo d'adieu de Cristiano Ronaldo a été diffusée dans l'auditorium, précédée d'un hommage de Pedro Proença, le président de la Fédération portugaise de football. Mais alors que le dirigeant fédéral était dans la confidence selon la presse portugaise, le sélectionneur national l'a découvert lui en même temps que tout le monde.

Le technicien lusitanien, sous contrat avec la Fédération portugaise de football jusqu'en 2030, avait initialement prévu d'intégrer l'attaquant de 41 ans dans son groupe de 28 joueurs. Jorge Jesus n'avait pas cherché à lui téléphoner en amont, persuadé que le joueur poursuivrait l'aventure lors de cette phase de poules. Une surprise totale. Face aux journalistes, l'entraîneur a raconté sans détour les coulisses de ce dénouement :

Cette annonce intervient un mois après de vives tensions au sein du vestiaire portugais. Lors du précédent rassemblement, Cristiano Ronaldo avait reproché à Jorge Jesus de ne pas l'avoir fait entrer face à la Norvège, avant de s'agacer de l'absence d'excuses en conférence de presse et de claquer la porte. Interrogé sur d'éventuels regrets concernant la gestion de cet épisode, le sélectionneur a balayé tout remords : « Rien. Zéro. Parce que j'ai pris une décision d'entraîneur. »

D'ailleurs, le sélectionneur a tenu à évacuer toute idée de conflit personnel avec celui qui met un terme à sa carrière internationale après 23 ans sous le maillot portugais, cumulant 234 sélections et 146 buts. « Si j'ai dit que j'envisageais de le convoquer, c'est parce que je pensais qu'il allait poursuivre le projet de Ligue des nations. Il n'y a aucune animosité », a rappelé le patron de la Seleção, assurant ne porter aucune culpabilité dans cette issue.

C'est un changement majeur pour la sélection portugaise. Afin d'assurer la continuité sportive et symbolique, Jorge Jesus a confirmé que le brassard de capitaine revenait à Bernardo Silva, joueur le plus capé du groupe présent, conformément à la tradition de la Seleção. Au-delà de ce passage de témoin, le sélectionneur a profité de ce rassemblement pour rajeunir son effectif. Ainsi, deux nouveaux éléments font leur apparition pour la première fois : le latéral droit Alberto Costa (FC Porto) et l'attaquant Gustavo Varela (Monza).

L'équipe nationale doit désormais se focaliser sur ses prochains rendez-vous sur le terrain. Le Portugal recevra le Danemark le samedi 14 novembre 2026 à 20h45 à l'Estádio Municipal de Braga pour la 5e journée de la Ligue des nations, avant d'affronter le pays de Galles le mardi 17 novembre 2026 à 20h45 au Cardiff City Stadium.

Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble canceled

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Free-to-play multiplayer arena shooter Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble has been canceled, publisher Sony Interactive Entertainment and developer Oh BiBi announced.

The game soft-launched for iOS and Android in the Philippines and Indonesia alongside its announcement in November 2025, followed by Norway, Sweden, Finland, and Denmark in December 2025, Canada in May 2026, and the United Kingdom in August 2026.

In a message posted to the game’s official website, Oh BiBi said a final update is being released today, and the game will be removed from storefronts on November 21. Servers will officially close on May 1, 2027.

■ Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble Update (November 6, 2026)

We’ve made the difficult decision to cease development of Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble.

The final planned update will be rolling out November 6, 2026, and the app will be removed from stores on November 21, 2026.

Players who already have the game will be able to continue playing until May 1, 2027, when the game’s servers will permanently shut down.

On behalf of the dev team and everyone helping to make the game, thanks to our amazing community for all your support, especially to those who have spent time playing, sharing feedback, and helping shape Ranger Rumble throughout its development!

Game servers will permanently shut down on May 1, 2027.

Can I continue playing after the game is removed from stores?

If you already installed the game, you will be able to continue playing the game until End of Service on May 1, 2027.

Will I still be able to download and reinstall the game?

If you’re an existing player, Yes, until November 21, 2026, after that the game will be delisted from Apple’s App Store and Google Play.

Will the game still work after the servers have been shut down?

No, once the game servers are shutdown on May 1, 2027, the game will no longer load.

Will there be any additional game updates?

No, there are no planned updates after November 7, 2026.

Will existing game modes, multiplayer matchmaking and competitive features remain available?

All current game modes, matchmaking and competitive features will remain active until End of Service on May 1, 2027.

Will there be any final events, rewards or opportunities to unlock content?

The Qwark Pass seasons will continue to run until May 1, 2027, and the Super Pass is now free, and all content can be earned throughout each season.

What happens to my Quark Pass and any unclaimed rewards?

You have until End of Service on May 1, 2027, to enter, claim and utilize any rewards earned through play.

No, in-app purchases have been removed. You can continue to earn rewards through play and spending that currency on upgrades, cosmetics, characters and currencies.

What happens to my unused in-game currency?

You have until End of Service on May 1, 2027, to use any currency you’ve earned or purchased.

Can I request a refund for previous purchases?

Your eligibility for a refund for any purchases made will be through Apple’s App Store or Google Play.

What happens to my account and game progress after shut-down?

All player data will be wiped from the servers at the End of Service, May 1, 2027. If you want to delete your data ahead of May 1, 2027, you can contact support here.

Can I transfer my progress, purchases or unlocked items to another game?

No, your progress and items are not transferable to any other game.

What happens if I registered for the game or earned pre-registration rewards?

Pre-registration rewards will be available to all players logging into the November 7, 2026 update.

What happens to my personal data after the game closes?

All player data will be deleted at End of Service, on May 1, 2027.

Does this affect other Ratchet & Clank games or PlayStation services?

No, Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble‘s ceasing development has no impact on other Ratchet & Clank games or PlayStation services.

Why aren’t XLM crypto traders betting big before the 23 November vote?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Stellar’s [XLM] next upgrade just cleared its testnet vote. Validators will decide on the mainnet version in a couple of weeks.

Hence, the question – Has the token’s price moved in lockstep?

Bell (also known as Protocol 30), cleared its testnet vote on 3rd November. Validators will vote on the mainnet version on the 23rd.

According to the Stellar Development Foundation, Bell would bring three changes. The first will involve letting smart contract tell payments for different customers apart. This would make life immensely easier for businesses.

The second change will allow smart contracts check post-quantum signatures as the network’s own signatures would stay the same. The last would switch ledger timing to milliseconds. Here, it’s worth noting that they’ve admitted that this change along won’t make blocks faster.

Also, 23rd is not the final launch date. In fact, Bell only goes live if validators approve it, with the Foundation clarifying that the date could still move.

In the meantime, wallets, exchanges and node operators need to update their software.

Ideally, an upgrade would put the price up on its shoulders. Not this one though.

XLM went up to about $0.171 on the 4th, then came tumbling down the next afternoon. It’s been stuck between $0.153 and $0.157 since. At press time, it was valued at around $0.155.

Indicators seemed to be indecisive. The RSI was at 45.2, under the Signal Line. The ADX on the DMI was at around 18.5. What did this indicate? Well, there didn’t seem to be a strong trend either way, though sellers were just slightly ahead.

Futures traders have mostly taken a few steps back too. Open interest dropped from about $94 million to $88 million during the slide and at the time of writing, it was $89.5 million. Funding was barely positive at 0.0011% too.

Simply put, it may be evident that everyone’s playing it safe. No one’s betting big before the big 23rd November vote. For now, $0.16 is the level to watch.

The AI boom’s missing balance-sheet question: What has actually been verified?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

GUEST COLUMN by Neil P. Osnato

I spent 13 years on the floor of the New York Stock Exchange. One lesson stayed with me long after the trading jackets disappeared: A transaction could be executed, documented and perfectly valid while the assumptions that made it attractive proved less durable than anyone expected.

That distinction matters in the artificial intelligence infrastructure boom. We can believe AI will transform productivity and still ask whether each financing, data-center expansion or long-term capacity agreement is supported by evidence that can survive the investment’s life.

The industry is making commitments across different clocks. Compute contracts may run for years. Debt can mature decades from now.

Servers have much shorter technology cycles. Power infrastructure, transmission upgrades and buildings may outlast several generations of the equipment they were built to support. Meanwhile, enterprise customers are still learning what they will use, how intensively they will use it and what they will pay.

None of this proves the investments are unsound. It does create a question that belongs beside revenue forecasts and capital budgets: What exactly has been verified at the moment an institution decides to rely on a promise?

An agreement to purchase compute can be commercially meaningful. It may support financing and demonstrate customer commitment. But it does not by itself establish that the underlying workloads will remain economically productive at the contracted scale, or that the customer will renew on similar terms once technology and prices change.

A serious review must separate the existence of the contract from the economic assumptions behind its duration, usage profile, counterparty capacity and renewal expectations. Contract enforceability and economic durability are related; they are not interchangeable.

A data-center campus can be announced, financed and partially constructed before all of its necessary electricity, cooling, network and equipment dependencies have become operational. A power purchase agreement is not itself proof of deliverability at the exact commissioning date. GPU orders are not proof that a facility can run the ordered cluster at the planned duty cycle.

For infrastructure planning, I use a simple sequence: requested load, evidenced load, deliverable load, energized load, utilized load and sustained demand. These are not six fancy names for the same megawatt. They describe different claims, each requiring different supporting records.

That distinction is already relevant to public grid decisions. Persistence Analytics Group has submitted comments in MISO’s Large Load Working Group proceedings on load forecasts, application and study processes, paired-resource studies and cost transparency. The recurring question is how planners preserve alignment when a proposed project’s capacity, timing or other material conditions change after an earlier study or commitment.

Financial engineering can provide meaningful protection. Minimum payments, parent guarantees, collateral requirements, termination provisions and residual-value commitments can shift or mitigate losses. They should be evaluated on their actual terms and credit strength.

But a contractual remedy is not the same as the continued usefulness of the underlying asset. A guarantee may support recovery from a counterparty while leaving the investor dependent on that counterparty’s resources, enforceability, timing and the value remaining after a disruption. Those are separate assumptions that deserve separate evidence.

This is especially important when apparently distinct exposures rely on a small set of interconnected customers, infrastructure providers, funding sources or technology assumptions. Diversifying contracts does not necessarily diversify the dependency underneath them.

Initial diligence usually asks whether a project is supportable at approval. A more durable approach asks what must remain true afterward — and which changes require the original case to be revisited before additional money is committed.

Consider a campus whose customer delays its ramp, whose power availability moves to a later year or whose equipment changes materially. Each event may be manageable on its own. But if the original financing or infrastructure decision assumed the earlier configuration, someone must decide whether the updated evidence still supports the next commitment.

That is a decision gate, not a prediction about whether AI succeeds. It calls for a documented chain: the representation being relied upon; the assumption embedded in it; the evidence supporting that assumption; the dependencies that must hold; the party bearing downside exposure; and the material change that would require reassessment.

Different specialists own different pieces. Engineers assess technical deliverability. Lawyers assess contractual rights. Accountants and finance teams assess financial statements and cash flows. Independent decision assurance asks whether those records, taken together, actually establish the proposition the capital allocator is relying on — and whether known gaps have been made explicit.

The productive-utilization debate is essential. So is the question of who will pay for AI intelligence, and at what price. But before authorizing the next investment, a chief financial officer or investment committee can ask an even more immediate question: Which assumptions are verified facts, which are binding commitments, which are conditional forecasts and which need to be revalidated because something material has changed?

A sector can produce transformational technology and disappointing returns on some of its assets. We have seen that distinction in markets before. The lesson is not to stop investing. It is to stop treating execution, delivery, utilization and durable economic value as if they were the same event.

On the trading floor, the confirmation of a trade was the beginning of understanding the position, not the end. AI infrastructure deserves the same discipline.

Neil P. Osnato is founder of Persistence Analytics Group LLC, an independent Decision Assurance™ and Infrastructure Assumption Verification™ firm. He previously spent 13 years trading on the New York Stock Exchange floor. His work focuses on the documentary evidence supporting consequential infrastructure and financial decisions. He wrote this article for SiliconANGLE.

Selected public background: MISO Large Load Working Group stakeholder feedback (LLWG-2026-7 and LLWG-2026-8), September 2026; The Bond Buyer, “Deciding About Data Centers,” October 28, 2026.

SHT Days 15, 16, 17: A New Hiking Partner and New Plan

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Today was a wonderful zero day. There was no hiking planned, though we did have a short list of things to do. We slow rolled it out of Lamb’s with a stop at the nearby bakery first. Baked treat and fancy coffee in hand, we started our drive back down to Duluth. We needed to retrieve my vehicle from the church parking lot. We will need it to shuttle ourselves to and from trailheads in the coming days. Bill was happy to have my company, as he just drove all the way up here yesterday by himself.

We ran a few errands together, then he dropped me off at the church and we began our separate drive back to Lambs. On our way back we made an ultra-important stop at Castle Danger Brewery, the one in Two Harbors. We spent a few chill hours there having a drink and food from the taco truck parked outside. Later this evening, we chilled by the fire back at Lamb’s to complete our day of relaxation.

After a much needed day of rest, today I got back after it. It was a beautiful day, and I had a treat in store for Bill. The iconic Bean and Bear Lakes are in the next section north of Penn Boulevard. Bill likes a good day hike, and if there’s one to do up here, this would be the one. He had the brilliant idea to go ahead and stash one vehicle at the Penn Boulevard trailhead last night on our way back from Duluth, as it was on our way to Lamb’s. We would make that our end point for today’s SOBO hike.

We started at the Tettegouche State Park trailhead, the one inside the park, and began our journey. The morning was cool, and we didn’t see any other hikers for the first few miles. We climbed up “the drainpipe”, a spot shown in videos of the past that is an incredibly steep rock scramble, but has been made much easier today with the addition of wooden steps.

Not the drainpipe, just another steep section with rock steps.

The day warmed up quickly, and I was just in pants and a t-shirt, though I could have easily worn shorts and been comfortable. Eventually we started seeing a ton of people. I mean hordes of hikers. I knew Bean and Bear Lake were probably very pretty, but today was a gorgeous fall Saturday, and the trees were at their autumn prime. Everybody and their mom was on trail today, and it was a shock to my system to see so many people after having the trail practically to myself for the past week.

Our first big views of this beautiful landscape.

Bill and I approached the main view of Bean and Bear Lakes (yes, very pretty!), squeezed in for a selfie, and departed. We hiked on to a less crowded area to sit down for a lunch break. I’m beginning to realize why the Penn Boulevard trailhead parking area is so large. This section of trail is incredibly popular for day hikers, and there are a handful of campsites in this section too.

Approaching the iconic Bean and Bear Lakes.

We continued on, constantly moving aside for oncoming traffic. Eventually we wound down to the trailhead where we were absolutely amazed to see that, not only had the parking lot filled up completely, but it had spilled out onto Penn Boulevard as well. Cars were lined up on both sides of the road as far as the eye could see. Wow! Good thing we parked our vehicle here last night!!

We hiked a little over 10 miles today. The terrain was surprisingly challenging for a section that so many people are willing to walk. To be fair, most people are only doing an out and back to Bean and Bear, which is probably more like 6 miles.

The plan today is for short mileage, then a bit of playtime later. We are on a vacation after all! Bill brought his golf clubs up here with the hopes of hitting some courses in good fall weather, and today is calling for just that.

Bill dropped me off at the Minnesota Highway 1 trailhead. He will pick me up at the Tettegouche trailhead we started at yesterday. Shortly after beginning my hike today, I find a puzzling sign before a three-way trail intersection. The sign says something like “SHT re-route” with an arrow. The arrow appears to point to the opposite side of the intersection, but there isn’t another sign on the other side confirming this. Furthermore, Avenza does not have the SHT heading that direction. At first, I do head down that path, but it’s severely downhill and I have reservations about this. Do I want to go downhill only to find out that this isn’t the right way and have to climb back out and backtrack? I vaguely remember something about a bridge being out across the river here, but I don’t remember if it’s been repaired or not.

I decide to just be safe and follow Avenza. At least this way I know I’ll make it! The trail follows the course of the Baptism River down to the Tettegouche State Wayside on Highway 61. The trail dips under the highway, then switches to pavement as it heads toward the Wayside, crosses the river on a bridge that cars drive on, and then basically follows the paved road back up the other side to the parking lot at the trailhead. Not the world’s most exciting 3.5 miles.

View from the bridge of the Baptism River emptying into Lake Superior.

My guess is that the trail bridge over the Baptism River had been repaired, but Avenza had not been updated to reflect this. It’s funny because the guidebook (or rather, databook) I’ve been using described the mileage between my two trailheads today as 1.4 miles. Yes, cutting across the river rather than going around it would definitely shorten the mileage. If I’m ever back in this area, I’m going to have to check out this bridge. It’s described as a swinging bridge over the highest falls entirely in Minnesota. I will definitely want to experience that. Did I just waste time on garbage miles this morning? Maybe. Nevertheless, I’m grateful I was healthy and able to enjoy a beautiful fall day outside.

Sometimes I see a tree that just begs to be photographed.

I found Bill at the trailhead and we drove to the local coffeeshop in Silver Bay. There’s not much in Silver Bay, but this coffeeshop was hopping and had delicious coffee and food. I highly recommend.

We continued on to the golf course in Silver Bay, where I played cheerleader to Bill and enjoyed the sunshine and gorgeous setting and weather conditions. I like to golf as well, but opted to just be a passenger today.  Today was essentially a zero, as the miles this morning were short and easy.

Beautiful weather was par for the course this weekend!

As I sat in the golf cart I came to the realization that my plan to complete the remaining mileage in the intended time frame was very aggressive. Especially considering I’ve already taken two zeros and tomorrow the weather is supposed to be terrible. If there was any chance of finishing, I would have needed to not take the two zeros I’ve already taken, and buckle down with serious mileage per day for the remainder, regardless of the weather. While this is definitely doable, I have my husband here, taking time away from his job, and I want him to enjoy his vacation as well. He’s willing to help me get this done, but I think the smart thing to do is to just intentionally slow down and pick a spot to stop and leave a small chunk of miles for some other time. In my opinion, even with a day pack 20+ mile days on the SHT is really ambitious. With this new plan I can hike shorter mileage and still sightsee and explore with Bill.

So, last year’s hike was derailed by an injury, and this year’s was derailed with intention. Who knows, pushing myself to the limits to finish might cause injury anyway. I am utterly not upset by this change. I still have miles to complete while I’m up here, and I’m going to make the best of it!

My name is Misty, and I hail from Nebraska, where I live with my husband, two cats, and flock of chickens. I worked in healthcare for over 16 years before giving it up for more outdoor pursuits. I hope to have you follow along on my new adventures!

AI Visibility Isn’t A Sham – Most Ways Of Measuring It Are via @sejournal, @gregjarboe

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Make AI visibility budgets defensible with AMEC's data quality checks, tests against customer behavior, and brand measures like recall and consideration.

If an AI visibility dashboard helped you win budget this year, read four datasets before your next quarterly review. Together, they show AI assistants send a sliver of the traffic Google sends. They also show that none of these datasets could isolate the hidden AI influence many of us assumed was turning up in Direct and branded search.

That doesn’t make AI visibility a sham. It means most of the ways we measure it wouldn’t pass a basic data quality test.

On Oct. 22, 2026, Rand Fishkin published a 5-Minute Whiteboard on the SparkToro Blog titled “Is AI Visibility a Marketing Boondoggle?” The next day, Amanda Natividad, SparkToro’s chief evangelist, sent her Audience Research Newsletter asking, “What if AI visibility is a sham?”

A long time ago, I learned to separate events from trends. Two warnings from co-authors of the same book, Zero Click Marketing, who work at the same company and publish a day apart, look more like one event than two.

But the data underneath their warnings came from other people. And it has been piling up.

Marie Haynes looked at 12 months of GA4 data across 100 websites. ChatGPT visitors converted at 13.8%, against 3.02% for Google organic. That’s more than 4.5 times better, and it’s the stat AI visibility vendors will quote.

But here’s the stat they won’t. Google still drove 96.5% of total conversions in her dataset, and Google organic drove 27 times more conversions than all LLMs combined. She also found that the exponential growth in ChatGPT sessions ended in August 2025, around the release of GPT-5.

SEO Works found a narrower edge. AI referrals converted at 7.18% versus 6.08% for organic, a pooled ratio of 1.18. Yet AI traffic converted worse on 23 of the 63 sites with enough volume to compare. For B2B product businesses, AI converted at 0.62 times the organic rate.

The line from that study I’d tape to every SEO manager’s monitor is this one. A 5% lift in organic search delivered 10.3 additional conversions per site per month. Doubling AI referral traffic delivered 6.6.

The usual rebuttal is that clicks were never the point. People see a brand in a ChatGPT answer, don’t click, and then search for it or type in the URL later.

Kevin Indig went looking for that. At first, eight of his 12 properties showed Direct traffic growing from August to September. Then he filtered for engaged sessions. Across the filtered January-September sample, total Direct rose 140.9%, while engaged Direct changed by −1.0%. On one site, GA4 recorded 409,000 Direct sessions, and only 198 were engaged.

He then ran 632 tests on four brands, comparing weekly AI visibility with Direct traffic and branded search at delays of zero to six weeks. When he shifted the data to deliberately wrong dates as a placebo check, several of those mismatched alignments correlated as strongly as the real ones. His conclusion: “An effect could still exist. I just couldn’t isolate it.”

Natividad tied this back to an Ahrefs study of 75,000 brands from May 2025. Branded web mentions correlated at 0.664 with AI Overview brand visibility, and branded anchors at 0.527. Branded search volume came in last at 0.392. If AI visibility reliably produced branded search, she argued, that number should be much higher.

See also: Direct Traffic & Popularity – Correlation, Not Causation

Neither of them said AI doesn’t matter. Fishkin wrote that AI is “probably quite influential on some buyers in some sectors.” He credited Mike King with the better frame, that AI is “more like a brand channel, a digital billboard if you will, and it should be invested-in and measured as such.”

Natividad put it more sharply. “Familiarity itself is a marketing outcome.” Then she added the sentence that matters most for anyone building a dashboard. “If we can’t prove it, we shouldn’t trust the attribution just because it seems right.”

The SEO world is arriving late to a conversation the PR measurement world started months ago.

On May 20, 2026, AMEC, the International Association for the Measurement and Evaluation of Communication, launched its GEO Principles at its Global Summit in Dublin. Two days later, I admitted that I’d been treating AI citations as the new search rankings and that framing was wrong. Appearing in an AI answer is an output, whether it changes what people understand, believe, or do is an outcome.

AMEC has also gone a layer deeper with a companion white paper, “AMEC Data Quality Principles: Tackling the Unstructured Data Quality Challenges in the AI Era.” Its lead author is Rob Key, founder and CEO of Converseon, and it was produced with the Global Data Quality Initiative, a coalition that includes the Insights Association, ESOMAR, and the Market Research Society.

The paper was written for social listening and media analysts, not SEOs. Read it with an AI visibility dashboard open in another tab anyway. It proposes four questions any data set should pass before anyone acts on it.

The third question is the one that should keep SEO directors up at night. AMEC calls it predictive and construct validity. A metric can be “internally consistent but externally untrue,” the paper warns, and in an AI era that kind of data “does not stay quarantined; it propagates.”

That is a precise description of most AI visibility scores. They’re consistent from week to week. They go up when you publish more. Whether they predict anything a business cares about is, after Indig’s 632 tests, still unproven.

Two more lines from the paper belong in every GEO pitch deck. Principle four says, “Evaluations performed once and stored on a shelf are decorative, not protective.” And its crosswalk with AMEC’s GEO framework says outputs are directional and should be tested “across tools, prompts, and time.” A single screenshot of ChatGPT recommending your brand is an anecdote.

Principle five goes further. It tells practitioners to avoid manipulative GEO, meaning content engineered to game generative AI rather than inform it. AMEC’s phrase for it is “promotion over precision.”

One caution in the spirit of the paper itself. Some of its most striking figures, including the claim that off-the-shelf sentiment models hover at 60% to 65% precision, come from a Converseon-sponsored benchmark, and Converseon sells the cure. The paper calls itself non-promotional, and I think its principles stand on their own. But apply its Source question to the paper, too.

I don’t think AI visibility is a boondoggle. I think the AI visibility score is.

The datasets above don’t prove AI has no influence. They prove we’ve been grading a brand channel with traffic-channel math, then filling the gap with scores nobody has validated against behavior. That’s how a reasonable idea turns into a budget line you can’t defend.

Mike King’s billboard frame is the right one, and it fits what I’ve been arguing in my columns on YouGov’s brand research. AI brands are winning on consideration before they win on trust. If AI answers work like a billboard for your brand, measure them the way brand marketers have measured billboards for decades, with exposure, recall, and consideration, and stop asking them to behave like a search result.

Demote AI referral traffic to a diagnostic. Keep reporting it. Stop setting targets on it. Then run the SEO Works math on your own GA4 property. Calculate what a 5% lift in organic conversions is worth next to doubling your AI referrals. If organic wins, as it did across their sample, your roadmap should say so.

Run your dashboard through AMEC’s four questions. Before an AI visibility number goes into a deck, document which tools and prompts produced it, whether results were logged in or logged out, and how bot sessions were filtered. Define whether you’re counting a mention, a citation, or a recommendation, because they aren’t interchangeable. If you can’t answer those, the number fails AMEC’s Source and Accuracy questions before anyone asks about results.

Test your score for real-world validity, Indig style. Pull weekly AI visibility for your brand and line it up against engaged Direct sessions, branded search, and pipeline at lags of one to six weeks. Then shift the series to wrong dates and run it again. If the wrong dates correlate as well as the right ones, you’ve learned your score isn’t predicting anything yet. That’s worth knowing before your vendor renewal.

Build a governed prompt library and re-run it on a schedule. Pick prompts that map to real buyer questions, not prompts that flatter your brand. Run them across several AI tools, save the outputs, and repeat monthly or quarterly. AMEC’s principle is that accuracy isn’t a point in time, and the same holds for visibility.

Ask people, then triangulate. Indig’s recommendation is self-reported attribution. Add a free-text “How did you hear about us?” field to demo and signup forms, and add AI assistants to your brand lift surveys. Natividad warns that this data has its own flaws, so treat it as one input. Her framework of exposure, behavior, and business outcomes is a sensible way to weigh them together.

Put money where the evidence overlaps. SEO Works found the sites gaining AI referrals were the same sites improving in organic, with a correlation of +0.557. Ahrefs found branded web mentions tracked AI Overview visibility better than branded search did. Earned authority does double duty. That’s the least glamorous budget line on your plan and, right now, the best supported one.

Fishkin asked whether AI visibility is a marketing boondoggle. My answer is that it becomes one the moment we report a number we haven’t checked against what customers actually do.

How Do You Land on a Moon With Almost No Gravity?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Japan’s MMX mission aims to sample the Martian moon Phobos

Japan's MMX spacecraft will fire its engines to slow down and enter orbit around Mars.

Last August, looking up from the edge of an ancient river delta on Mars, the Perseverance rover’s electronic eye spied an oblong shadow crossing the distant, glowing Sun. Phobos, the Martian moon named for the Greek god of fear and panic, was transiting the solar disk. Too small to darken the pale, dust-tinged sky, but orbiting close enough to eclipse a significant fraction of the Sun, Phobos passed in less than a minute.

NASA’s Perseverance rover captured Phobos passing in front of the Sun from the surface of Mars.ASU/MSSS/SSI/JPL-Caltech/NASA

The tiny moon, with its murky origins, is an enigma, and one whose makeup and history could tell us much about Mars and the wider solar system. And now the international Martian Moons eXploration (MMX) mission, which successfully launched on 20 October, seeks to explore that mystery with an unprecedented series of landings on the unknown surface of Phobos.

This mission brings unusual challenges—for example, designing an orbit to study a microgravity moon orbiting a much larger planet. Engineers also had to figure out how to assess and land on this low-gravity pile of loosely assembled dirt and rock, how a rover might rove across it, and how the spacecraft might take samples and return them to Earth.

“I often say that landing on Phobos is like trying to land a balloon,” says Yasuhiro Kawakatsu, project manager of MMX at the Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA). “It’s very difficult to drop a balloon to the ground and prevent it from tilting or bouncing.”

Phobos is one of two small Martian moons; the other is Deimos, named after the Greek god of dread and terror. Their cosmic origins are unknown, though there are two prevailing theories. One holds that the moons were once asteroids from the belt between Mars and Jupiter, captured by the smaller planet’s gravity. In the other theory, Phobos and Deimos formed when a large celestial body slammed into Mars, sending debris into orbit. Some models suggest the pair are the last survivors from a cluster of satellites.

The enormous Stickney crater, roughly 9 kilometers across, dominates this view of Phobos taken by NASA’s Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter.University of Arizona/JPL-Caltech/NASA

Scientifically speaking, Phobos is considered a rubble pile, a loosely consolidated aggregate of rock and dust. Far too small to become spherical under its own mass, it’s shaped like a lumpy potato, roughly 27 kilometers in length, with only 1/1,800th of Earth’s gravity. On Phobos, a typical adult would weigh about as much as a tennis ball does on Earth.

More than 10 years in the making, MMX launched on Japan’s flagship H3 rocket from Tanegashima spaceport on 20 October local time. It will arrive at Mars in 2027 carrying scientific payloads from several space agencies, including NASA, and a new rover called Idefix developed by the French and German space agencies (CNES and DLR) in collaboration with JAXA.

Engineers assembled MMX’s modules at Japan’s Tanegashima Space Center before the spacecraft’s October 2026 launch.JAXA

MMX consists of three modules: a propulsion module to power its journey to Mars and apply the brakes to enter orbit around the red planet; an exploration module carrying the rover, sampling gear, and other scientific instruments; and a return module, attached to the exploration module, that harbors the capsule destined for a high-speed reentry into Earth’s atmosphere. After collecting samples on Phobos, the exploration and return modules will lift off together. The exploration module will then be jettisoned while the return module sets a course back home.

If all goes according to plan, MMX will deliver a 10-gram sample to Earth in the second half of 2031. But the mission will face a series of daunting challenges to reach that end point, including navigating complex orbits around Phobos and landing on its surface while Mars periodically blocks communications with Earth.

The MMX’s modular design includes the propulsion module [left] and the exploration and return modules [right].Kyodo News/Getty Images

“It’s the most complex mission I have ever seen,” says Patrick Michel, director of research at CNRS (the French National Center for Scientific Research) and co-principal investigator on Idefix.

An H3 rocket carried MMX into space on 20 October 2026, beginning the mission’s five-year journey to Phobos and back.The Asahi Shimbun/Getty Images

Once MMX reached low Earth orbit, its propulsion module’s engine fired, kicking off a nearly year-long deep-space cruise to Mars. On arrival, the spacecraft will need to fire its engines in an extended, carefully timed burn to slow down enough to be captured by Mars gravity. The propulsion module will then be jettisoned, leaving the exploration and return modules to orbit Mars.

“If something doesn’t go according to plan, MMX will fly by Mars and get catapulted into the solar system,” says Nicola Baresi, an astrodynamicist who coauthored a paper on specially designed orbits for MMX.

From that initial orbit, MMX will transition into a specially designed quasi-satellite orbit that allows it to keep pace with the small moon but also move around it. Since Phobos is too small to orbit in the conventional sense, the spacecraft will orbit Mars alongside the moon, following a clever egg-shaped path that slings MMX alternately ahead of and behind the moon. “When you’re closer to Mars, you’re accelerating with respect to Phobos, and when you’re farther from Mars, you’re decelerating. So you essentially go back and forth,” explains Baresi.

Martian Maneuvers To reach Phobos, MMX will execute three major maneuvers: MOI-1 will put the spacecraft into orbit around Mars, MOI-2 will transfer it toward Phobos, and MOI-3 will place it into its final orbit alongside the moon.Chris Philpot

To reach Phobos, MMX will execute three major maneuvers: MOI-1 will put the spacecraft into orbit around Mars, MOI-2 will transfer it toward Phobos, and MOI-3 will place it into its final orbit alongside the moon.

The best existing images of Phobos, from spacecraft such as NASA’s Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter and ESA’s Mars Express, have resolutions of only a few meters per pixel. That’s larger than Idefix, the mission’s rover, itself about the size of a microwave oven. To map the surface in greater detail, MMX will loop in front of and behind Phobos at distances ranging from 200 kilometers to less than 20 km from the surface.

During its two-year remote sensing campaign, the exploration module will use several cameras and a laser altimeter to map the surface in high resolution and look for candidate landing sites. When Mars passes behind the Sun as seen from the Earth, causing a roughly two-week communications blackout, MMX will retreat into a larger, stable orbit. After communications resume, MMX will prepare to send its first visitor down to the surface.

Before the exploration and connected return modules attempt to land, Kawakatsu says, MMX will send a scout that in Japanese is called “the first penguin,” leaping into unknown waters. For this mission, the first to leap will be the Idefix rover, named for the canine companion to Obelix in the French comics series Asterix. The exploration module will release the rover during a low-altitude pass of between 40 and 100 meters above Phobos’s surface in 2028 or early 2029.

MMX will use its TENGOO camera [left] and LIDAR laser altimeter [right] to map Phobos’s terrain.JAXA

It will be a tense moment that calls back to 2005, during Japan’s Hayabusa mission to explore the asteroid Itokawa. In a similarly designed drop, the MINERVA rover was released from an orbiter, but erroneously at 200 meters instead of the intended 70 meters. The rover missed the asteroid and, without propulsion, drifted off into deep space.

Idefix will get a slight nudge out of the exploration module toward its negligible-gravity target, freefalling and likely tumbling as it goes. It’s expected to touch down with a velocity of less than 1 meter per second. Unlike rovers that have landed on other surfaces with airbags or dedicated landing legs, Idefix will land without protection, apart from some extra padding on the solar panels due to their critical nature. The barely-there gravity of Phobos should allow the 23-kilogram rover to land softly, though exactly how this landing will play out is hard to predict.

The Idefix rover will travel to Phobos with its wheels tucked in and solar panels folded.DLR

“It will bounce probably several times,” says Stephan Ulamec, Idefix’s co-principal investigator and project manager at DLR. “It will come to rest somehow in some position.” Once it’s still, four retracted legs will deploy to right the rover autonomously, regardless of its orientation. It will then unfold its solar panels to charge its batteries for exploration tasks ahead.

A simulation shows how Idefix is expected to tumble onto Phobos, right itself using its articulated legs, and begin exploring the moon’s surface. DLR

Once operational, Idefix will roll at a snail’s pace, topping out at one millimeter per second. Its metal wheels have protruding blades like a paddle wheel, designed to gain traction in Phobos’s microgravity. “If we were to drive our rover faster, then it would actually be more like we were swimming through instead of driving across the surface,” says Cecily Sunday, a doctoral student involved in testing Idefix’s WheelCams at the Institut Superieur de l’Aeronautique et de l’Espace (ISAE-Supaéro) in Toulouse, France.

The WheelCams, a pair of cameras placed on the underside of the rover, will observe the wheels and their tracks during Idefix’s planned 100-Earth-day primary mission. The images will help researchers study the size and distribution of grains in Phobos’s regolith and how cohesive they are. How much or how little the grains stick together will greatly impact how Idefix moves. Sunday likens this to the difference between driving on dry and wet sand. “Think of trying to build a sandcastle with dry sand,” she says. “You can’t. Add water, and suddenly you can.”

The Idefix rover was mounted between the MMX spacecraft’s landing legs for the journey to Phobos.DLR

The team tested the rover’s nine-bladed wheels on a variety of materials, including Phobos regolith simulants provided by JAXA and DLR, lunar simulants, glass beads, quartz sand, and clay pebbles. “We characterize the mechanical properties of each simulant precisely, then test across the full range, with varying grain size, angularity, and even color, so we can map what we see in the WheelCam images back to actual surface properties,” Sunday says.

Despite the microgravity and Phobos’s rubble-pile nature, Sunday says Earth-based tests can approximate the rover’s motion on the surface of the small moon fairly well. While Phobos’s reduced gravity lowers the rover’s effective weight, there is also reduced pressure between the grains beneath its wheels. “As long as you’re moving very, very slowly and using the same materials and the same weight of the system, they should be representative,” Sunday says.

With four paddle-like wheels and deployable solar panels, Idefix is designed to crawl across Phobos.Ardan Fuessmann/Imago/Alamy

Idefix’s interactions with its new home will offer “sub-millimeter resolution and information on porosity, on grain size, on surface strength, on how hard is the material,” Ulamec says. “It fills a gap between remote observations from orbit and detailed observations of the return samples back on Earth.”

What Idefix finds on this rocky, rugged world will help determine how and where JAXA attempts to land the main spacecraft. Michel says the touchdown will provide ground truth for understanding of Phobos. When NASA’s OSIRIS-REx spacecraft touched down on the asteroid Bennu to collect a sample, its sampling arm surprisingly sank nearly half a meter into the loose material. But Ulamec doesn’t think Idefix will sink deep into Phobos, noting that Bennu is very different and much smaller, with a diameter of 500 meters.

Once Idefix has investigated the surface, MMX will face an even greater challenge: landing its much larger exploration module on Phobos. The spacecraft will make a controlled descent to roughly 10 meters above the surface, then drop the remaining distance in free fall. With so little gravity to pull it downward, even a small sideways motion could cause the lander to tip or bounce away. A successful, upright landing would mark the start of up to two hours on Phobos for the crucial sampling phase.

The MMX mission draws on the experience and technologies of Japan’s two Hayabusa sample-return missions, which landed on the asteroids Itokawa and Ryugu. But this mission has many more moving parts. MMX’s exploration module will use two sampling devices—a coring sampler developed by JAXA and a pneumatic sampler provided by NASA and fabricated by Honeybee Robotics, a subsidiary of Blue Origin. Data from these tools, and those carried by the Idefix rover, could answer questions about the origin of Phobos and how the Mars system has evolved.

The coring sampler uses a robot arm and a cylindrical corer designed to reach at least 2 centimeters below the surface. The arm can maneuver to find the best site for digging. “That by itself is a challenge because we’ve never done it,” Michel says. “You never know what can happen at the very sampling site itself, where you might have a boulder and the arm cannot move.”

Probing beneath the top layer will be valuable because solar radiation, galactic cosmic rays, and micrometeorites have battered and weathered the surface. The material below should offer a better record of the moon’s origins, and JAXA aims to collect at least 10 grams of it.

MMX will carry both the Idefix rover [bottom] and a robotic coring sampler [top] designed to dig beneath Phobos’s weathered surface.JAXA

The pneumatic sampler takes a different approach. Mounted on one of the lander’s legs, it will inject pure, non-reactive nitrogen gas into the surface and capture the grains stirred up. This sample could illuminate the changes Mars and its moons have undergone over time. The dual system also adds redundancy in the face of Phobos’s unknowns. “If the surface of Phobos proves to be very hard, [the coring sampler] may struggle to penetrate the ground. [The pneumatic sampler] would not experience this issue and still be able to gather material,” Kawakatsu says.

One of the most intriguing aspects of MMX is the additional possibility of collecting material from the planet below. It’s likely that Phobos has been peppered by Martian ejecta thrown into space by meteor impacts, so MMX also provides an opportunity to return the first Martian samples to Earth. Michel estimates that the samples could contain “a few hundred grains coming from Mars.” Scientists can distinguish those grains from native Phobos material by analyzing their oxygen isotopes.

That possibility raises a planetary-protection question. A direct Mars sample-return mission would have to follow strict rules to ensure that potentially living biological material from Mars doesn’t contaminate Earth. But those protocols don’t apply to MMX, even if its sample does collect grains of Martian soil. A panel of experts concluded that any microbes blasted off Mars wouldn’t survive the violent impact and ejection or the intense radiation exposure on the way to Phobos. This saved MMX from implementing expensive and time-consuming quarantine and containment protocols for the returning sample.

After landing on Phobos, MMX will have roughly two hours to collect samples from the moon’s surface before lifting off again.JAXA

During the brief surface operation, the robotic arm will deposit the samples in a storage unit, from which they’ll be transferred into a capsule aboard the return module and sealed in a vacuum. After that, lift off.

Once back in Mars orbit, the exploration module will resume its observations of Phobos and make several flybys of Mars’s other diminutive moon, Deimos, to gather data on its surface, structure, and composition. In November 2030, during a favorable alignment of Mars and Earth, MMX will begin the homeward leg of its trip. The return module will separate from the exploration module and power the voyage home, its sole objective to bring the dust of Phobos to Earth.

MMX’s sample return capsule will carry material from Phobos through Earth’s atmosphere in 2031.JAXA

Finally, in 2031, the 60-centimeter-wide sample capsule will plunge into Earth’s atmosphere at roughly 12 kilometers per second. Assuming it survives the extreme heat of reentry and lands safely in Australia, scientists will begin to tease out clues about Phobos and the early solar system. JAXA’s Institute of Space and Astronautical Science will receive and curate the samples, which will also be shared with mission partners.

“We have macroscale measurements from the spacecraft, microscale measurements with the rover, and then sample analysis,” Michel says. “So we can relate the three kinds of scales together because we have all of them.” The insights gleaned from orbital images, close-up images of the regolith, and the chemical analysis of the samples could have wider implications for our understanding of our solar system.

“MMX is a mission to understand the Mars system and shed light onto how terrestrial planets like the Earth, and maybe Mars, develop a habitable environment,” Kawakatsu says. “We strongly suspect that our terrestrial planet region formed dry, and water and organics were scattered inwards from the icy-rich outer solar system.” Mars, he says, sits at the gateway between the inner and outer solar system, making its moons valuable witnesses to the processes that shaped potentially habitable worlds.

Andrew Jones is a freelance journalist based near Helsinki. He writes about the space industry and technology with a particular focus on China's activities.

The communications blackout offers a chance to learn things about handling errors autonomously and recording and storing data autonomously that should useful for other deep space missions. I wonder what the engineers are most worried about for that phase of the mission!

Quiz question: what was unique about Tony Cottee’s goal for West Ham against Charlton, in the FA Cup third round in January 1986? Answer: during that domestic season, it was the first live televised goal in either league or cup.

There hadn’t been loads of goalless draws before that – actually, there had been a complete TV blackout. No live football, not even Match of the Day highlights, after a dispute between the Football League and the broadcasters, BBC and ITV.

Even when tensions thawed, only 13 English club games were shown live during the second half of that campaign.

Fast forward 40 years, and you’d struggle to find a time where there weren’t at least 13 domestic league games televised in a single weekend. This season, a minimum of 267 Premier League matches will be shown live on Sky and TNT, plus 1,000 games from the EFL, League Cup and EFL Trophy, with the FA Cup and European games on top of that.

That’s just one example of how football has changed over the years – new state-of-the-art arenas have also been built around the country.

The poisoned public rights doctrine and Sun Valley Orchards

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

On Tuesday, Nov. 10, the court will hear argument in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards, LLC. This case concerns the H-2A visa program for temporary alien immigrant workers. The issue is whether the Department of Labor’s Article II administrative law judges have the authority to collect ruinous fines from employers who violate their employment contracts with such individuals.

In early August of this year, I signed an amicus brief supporting Sun Valley Orchards along with former Attorneys General Edwin Meese III and Michael Mukasey and law professor Gary Lawson, prepared and filed by our lawyer Amit Vora. We argued that the respondent was entitled not only to a Seventh Amendment civil jury but also to an Article III federal court.

After reading the government’s opening and reply briefs by Solicitor General D. John Sauer, I stand by our argument that a DOL administrative law judge may not deprive Sun Valley of property by imposing ruinous fines for violating a contract that bound it to follow certain labor laws in employing foreign immigrant H-2A workers. Rather, Sun Valley has a Seventh Amendment right to have its claims heard by a jury if it desires to do so.

The government is obviously correct that the government has a sovereign right to control immigration into the United States under the H-2A program. It does not follow from that, however, that the government can condition the admission of foreign workers under this program on Sun Valley’s surrendering its constitutional right to have employment disputes with those workers adjudicated by Article III courts with a Seventh Amendment right to a jury trial. Constitutional rights cannot be taken away without due process of law or in violation of, for example, the First, Fourth, or Seventh amendments. But that is exactly what the government is trying to do in this case.

By way of example, in 1987’s Nollan v. California Coastal Commission, the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Antonin Scalia, held that the California Coastal Commission could not use its plenary power over zoning to condition a renovation construction permit on the Nolans ceding a public easement to walk along their valuable California coastal property, as doing so would violate the takings clause of the Fifth Amendment. Likewise, the government cannot impose unconstitutional conditions when using either its zoning power or its plenary power over immigration. Nor can the government use its commerce power or any of its other enumerated powers to violate one’s constitutional rights. The government can shut down the foreign immigrant H2A program. But it cannot use it as a cudgel to make Americans surrender their constitutional rights simply because the rights of immigrants are at stake.

Central to the government’s argument otherwise is the public-rights doctrine. This doctrine – properly understood – allows for privileges created by the government to be adjudicated by non-Article III judges, such as the privileges to receive social-security, disability, or welfare payments; the privilege to immigrate into the United States; or the privilege to receive tax rebates under the federal government’s partial waiver of sovereign immunity. With regard to such privileges, the Constitution’s guarantee of an Article III forum and the Seventh Amendment civil jury trial right are inapplicable because the receipt of these were not property rights recognized at common law, but privileges created by the government. The government argues that because immigration into the country is also a privilege, not a right, Sun Valley’s conduct related to this are not subject to Article III protections.

It is true that the public rights doctrine was profoundly altered (one could say poisoned), in the 1977 case of Atlas Roofing Co. v. Occupational Safety and Health Administration. In Atlas Roofing, Justice Byron White denied access to an Article III court and a Seventh Amendment civil jury trial to a party subject to an OSHA enforcement order. According to White, because such orders were based on “new statutory obligations,” they could be “committed exclusively to an administrative agency [for] deciding whether a violation has in fact occurred.” This was incorrect: while OSHA created new torts that can occur in the workplace, these were analogous to the kinds of tort actions that would have been heard at common law. Indeed, such torts are classic “Cases in Law,” which Article III gives sole jurisdiction over to life-tenured federal judges assisted by a civil jury if the plaintiffs choose one. Thus, Atlas Roofing Co. changed the law to say that the public-rights doctrine not only allowed Article II administrative law judges to deprive persons of privileges, but, for the first time, it also eliminated the guarantee of a Seventh Amendment jury trial when a person or entity was deprived of property rights by the government.

Fortunately, this dangerous precedent was limited by the more recent 2024 case of SEC v. Jarkesy. In Jarkesy, the Supreme Court held that when the SEC imposed civil penalties against a defendant for securities fraud, the Seventh Amendment entitled the defendant to a jury trial. This is because in 1787 a fraud case, like the employment contract case at issue in Sun Valley (or, for that matter, the property seized in Atlas Roofing), would have been “Case in Law” assigned to the sole jurisdiction of the Article III courts.

So the lesson of Jarkesy is this: While the vast majority of cases which ALJs decide only “privileges” and not “rights” only require an Article II hearing, when federal regulatory agencies impose ruinous fines on a corporation or person, those fines are deprivations of one’s right to property. And in depriving one of such property, it is not enough to have an administrative hearing: The case must be heard by an Article III court with the right to a Seventh Amendment civil-jury trial.

The public-rights doctrine then does not apply to enforcement orders that deprive a person of property by agencies such as OSHA in Atlas Roofing, the SEC in Jarkesy, or the DOL in Sun Valley. Nor does calling a case an immigration matter empower the government to take away the constitutional rights of Americans even if immigration into the United States is itself a privilege and not a right.

So let’s hope that the Supreme Court in Sun Valley rejects, as unconstitutional, the DOL’s attempt to funnel a private-rights dispute away from an Article III forum. And let’s further hope that the court clearly reframes the public-rights doctrine, as limited in Jarkesy, as a mere manifestation of the rights/privileges distinction – and continues to prevent the doctrine from taking the form that Justice White allowed for in Atlas Roofing: an ominous threat to Article III, the Seventh Amendment, and the due process clause.

Professor Steven Gow Calabresi is the Clayton J. & Henry R. Barber Professor of Law at Northwestern Pritzker School of Law.

Recommended Citation: Steven Gow Calabresi, The poisoned public rights doctrine and Sun Valley Orchards, SCOTUSblog (Nov. 6, 2026, 10:00 AM), https://www.scotusblog.com/2026/11/the-poisoned-public-rights-doctrine-and-sun-valley-orchards/
