This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

“While they loved the historic feel and comfortable size of the main home for their family, they needed extra space to relax and entertain, and privacy to host guests,” explains Underwood. “We planned for what they need now but also anticipated the future.”

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: ARBO Landscape Architects Location: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dov on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

The iPad Pro is my least favourite iPad, and that's because I'm not a video editor or professional digital artists, so I have no need for its immense power. But then, even when you consider its potential, I just feel that it's a heavy tablet that's way over-priced. The Air and the classic iPad will always appeal over the Pro for me.

Grow These 6 Beautiful Living Christmas Trees in Pots – Then Enjoy Them in Your Garden for Years

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A real Christmas tree doesn't have to be disposable. Choose the right variety, and your festive centerpiece can have a whole new life outdoors.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If there is one piece of decor that represents the holidays, it’s got to be a Christmas tree. Add an evergreen tree to your living room, toss on some tinsel, and, suddenly, it’s the holidays.

But Christmas trees seem more expensive every year, and it’s not fun to spend all that money on a one-use tree that you'll discard in January. This is the year to switch to a beautiful live Christmas tree that lasts for years. There are lots of miniature Christmas-type trees that can be grown in pots, spruced up for the holiday, then enjoyed in the garden for the rest of the year.

The key is to buy a living tree that is attractive and built for the long haul, not a single use. I’ve got you covered on that score with a short list of the best living Christmas tree options, from miniature traditional-looking spruces to unusual blue and golden conifers.

What’s the best small evergreen tree to grow in a pot for Christmas? Well, the answer will be different for each of us. There are plenty of attractive trees that make good live Christmas trees through December, then live happily in the garden the rest of the year. Pick your favorite, but make sure it’s suited to your USDA hardiness zone so it can thrive outdoors for years to come!

Dwarf Alberta spruce (Picea glauca 'Conica') makes wonderful live, potted Christmas trees. These trees offer a classic pyramidal Christmas tree shape and incredible longevity – to 50 years or more in the right conditions. Their compact, densely packed growth habit makes these trees look cute, and they grow very slowly – between 2 and 4 inches (2.5–5 cm) per year – and top out at just over 10 feet (3 m) tall and wide. The needles are fine-textured and soft to the touch, a rich blue-green. They are also aromatic when rubbed.

You can order a classic dwarf Alberta spruce online at Nature Hills, or for something a little different, the Rainbow's End cultivar, which produces a second flush of lemon-yellow growth in summer.

These little spruces are easy to care for, drought-tolerant, and cold-tolerant. They grow best in well-drained acidic soil and prefer full sun. Only water when the top 3 inches of soil are dry. Their preferred climate (USDA zones 2–8) has cold winters and cool summers.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

A dwarf blue spruce (Picea pungens 'Globosa') can make a great compact, living Christmas tree. You can doll it up for the holidays indoors, then move this slow-growing evergreen conifer outdoors for the winter in USDA zones 2–8. It needs little maintenance and offers a tidy, manageable size and striking silver-blue foliage.

The signature blue needles make the dwarf spruce a unique holiday tree, and its strong, stiff branches hold up heavy ornaments without issue. The natural pyramidal form requires little shaping, and its needles rarely fall out. On the other hand, the needles are prickly and can hurt you when you are decorating if you aren’t careful. Plus, this spruce doesn’t offer the beloved evergreen aroma, but a distinct odor that isn’t universally loved.

Dwarf blue spruce trees require very little attention once established. When you are moving the tree outside, put it in a site with lots of direct sun. Add several inches of organic mulch to the soil. The ‘Globosa’ cultivar, available from Woodies Garden Goods, reaches 3 to 5 feet (1–1.5 m) tall and wide.

Korean fir (Abies koreana) is a compact, slow-growing, symmetrical evergreen tree. With its soft, rounded needles and dense growth habit, it is a popular choice for a live Christmas tree.

The needles are very ornamental, a dark green with a silvery underside, and offer a rich, fresh forest scent with notes of citrus. The cones are special too, a striking violet-purple and stand upright. This slow-growing fir creeps up a bit every year to finish at some 15-30 feet tall, with a conical form. A species tree will grow to 30 feet (10 m) tall, but a dwarf variety like Cis Dwarf Korean Fir, from Nature Hills, stops at 2 to 3 feet tall and wide. For something different, Ice Breaker Korean Fir is a dwarf variety that looks perpetually snow-covered.

Plant in full sun to partial shade in moist, well-drained, acidic soil. These trees thrive in USDA zones 5–7.

A dwarf Norway spruce (Picea abies) makes a charming, compact live Christmas tree that can be kept in a pot or, in zones 3–7, planted in the garden after the holidays. These small trees typically reach around 3 feet (1 m) tall and wide at maturity, depending on the cultivar. They make charming, sustainable live Christmas trees and can be moved outdoors after the festive season.

A dwarf Norway spruce tree offers a classic triangular shape, dark green needles, and graceful, drooping branchlets. This classic Christmas tree is popular for its dense, symmetrical shape and rich "Christmassy" scent that quickly fills up the room. Unfortunately, Norway spruce needles can dry out and drop fast in warm indoor temperatures, so keep it in as cool a spot as possible.

These trees are generally about 3 feet tall (90 cm) when sold as potted Christmas trees, and the ultimate height depends on the cultivar. They grow slowly, adding between 1 and 3 inches (5–10 cm) every year.

You can buy both ‘Pumila’ dwarf Norway spruce and ‘Pusch’ dwarf Norway spruce at Nature Hills. ‘Pumila’ has a dense, bushy shape that looks lovely decorated for Christmas, while ‘Pusch’ offers something a little different, producing tiny red cones in spring that add extra interest to the garden.

Japanese cedars (Cryptomeria japonica) make unique live Christmas trees with soft, fine foliage that is pleasant to touch. The appearance depends on the cultivar, but many compact cultivars offer a naturally dense, rounded shape. The foliage can take on bronze, copper, or bluish-green tones during winter.

For a real statement tree, Black Dragon Japanese cedar, available from Plant Addicts, has a mature height of 8–10 feet (2.5–3 m), or for a smaller tree, the Tansu Dwarf Japanese cedar, from Nature Hills, reaches 3–4 feet (1–1.2 m).

Caring for your live Japanese cedar tree isn't difficult, but you’ll want to limit how much time it spends indoors. Too much time in a warm house can make the tree unhappy. Indoors, keep the tree away from direct heat sources. When it’s time to take the tree outside, start it out in a sheltered spot or an unheated garage for a few days.

Be sure to keep the soil moist. Check it every day. Japanese cedars thrive in full sun to partial shade in USDA zones 5–9 and require well-drained, fertile soil. Standard varieties can grow to 50 feet (15 m) tall if planted in the ground. Dwarf cultivars stay much smaller.

You will recognize the lemon cypress (Cupressus macrocarpa ‘Goldcrest’) – it’s the potted evergreen that you are likely to see in every store just before Christmas as a holiday plant. You can even get one from Walmart. It makes a charming, small Christmas tree with its natural pyramid shape, bright chartreuse foliage, and fresh citrus scent.

The lemon cypress is a versatile evergreen that, once the festive season is done, can be kept in a container on a patio in USDA zones 7–10. Be gentle with it indoors. Use lightweight ornaments since the soft branches can bend or break under heavier decorations. You’ll want to avoid hot lights, too. Use battery-operated micro fairy lights that don’t produce excess heat.

These trees grow slowly and can top out at 10 feet (3 m) tall and 3 feet (1 m) wide. Indoors, give them a sunny window with 6 hours of bright light a day. Keep the tree far from hot air vents and cool drafts, and provide a pebble tray for humidity. Transition the cypress to an outdoor location after the holidays.

Teo Spengler is a master gardener and a docent at the San Francisco Botanical Garden, where she hosts public tours. She has studied horticulture and written about nature, trees, plants and gardening for more than two decades. Before turning her focus to gardening, she worked as an attorney and legal writer, experience she also brings to her role as our Gardening Lawyer. Her extended family includes some 30 houseplants and hundreds of outdoor plants, including 250 trees, which are her main passion. Spengler currently splits her life between San Francisco and the French Basque Country, though she was raised in Alaska, giving her experience of gardening in a range of climates.

Democrats won control of Congress. So what does that mean for the Supreme Court?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Democrats won big in Tuesday’s midterm elections, flipping at least 40 seats in the House and at least seven in the Senate. Come January, the Democratic Party will control both houses of Congress, which will allow it to set policymaking priorities and play a lead role in shaping the future of the Supreme Court.

Most immediately, the power shift in the Senate will complicate (if not effectively eliminate) President Donald Trump’s ability to appoint another justice to the Supreme Court, should a vacancy arise after the new Congress is seated. The Democratic Party will have enough votes to reject a nomination – if it even allows confirmation hearings to proceed.

Kelsey Dallas is SCOTUSblog's managing editor and the primary author of the SCOTUStoday newsletter.

Asset Based Lending closes $200M securitization

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Asset Based Lending closed its largest deal, a $200M unrated securitization backed by business-purpose residential loans. ABL 2026 RTL-1 allows up to 65% ground-up infill construction collateral and brings cumulative securitization volume to $723M.  AI Summary

Asset Based Lending (ABL), a private real estate lender, announced on Tuesday that it closed a $200 million securitization, its largest to date, backed by business-purpose residential real estate loans, including a significant concentration of ground-up construction loans.

The transaction, ABL 2026 RTL-1, priced Oct. 15 and closed Oct. 26. It marks the company’s fourth unrated securitization and brings its cumulative securitization volume to $723 million.

Up to 65% of the collateral pool is eligible to consist of ground-up infill construction loans, a segment that has been central to ABL’s lending strategy since 2014.

The transaction came amid volatility in the securitization market and increased investor scrutiny of credit risk, said Daniel Gotay, ABL’s head of capital markets. Despite these conditions, the company secured what he described as some of the tightest pricing levels for an unrated issuer.

“What is most exciting about this one is that we’re not just doing our fourth, but that the market was a tricky market this time around,” Gotay said in an interview with HousingWire. “For us to execute at one of the sharpest levels for an unrated issuer, and to do that with the highest concentration of ground-up construction, really spoke to the presence that we’ve worked hard to build on Wall Street.”

Gotay said investors were particularly focused on how lenders would manage credit and construction risks over the next two years. ABL completed more than 20 virtual investor meetings ahead of the transaction, highlighting its underwriting and servicing practices.

The company services its loans in-house and uses a high-touch approach to managing projects, Gotay said. He added that investors also evaluated ABL’s broader portfolio, which has maintained a weighted average credit score of 740 and a weighted average loan-to-value ratio of 64%.

ABL has kept its weighted average LTV in the low 60% range for much of the past decade, Gotay said, contrasting that with some competing securitizations featuring LTV ratios in the upper-60% to low-70% range.

Investor appetite for the asset class remains strong despite heightened caution, Gotay said. International capital, including funds based in the United Kingdom and Japan, is increasingly looking to invest in U.S. asset-backed securities. “Even though they’re a little more on guard because of what’s happening, they still have to deploy their money for this type of asset,” he said.

Kevin Rodman, CEO of Asset Based Lending, said the transaction reflects the company’s experience in financing residential construction.

“We are proud to lead the industry in financing ground-up construction, and to do it at a cost that keeps our pricing competitive for the builders we serve,” Rodman said in a statement. “This transaction underscores the strength of the platform our team has built and the confidence investors have in our credit. Every one of these loans puts new housing on the ground.”

Gotay said ground-up construction has long been a core part of ABL’s business and will remain a significant share of its lending activity.

“The firm is really built to manufacture and manage infill ground-up construction since, really, 2014,” he said. “That’s been our specialty in the market and where we win the most deals.”

Performance Trust Capital Partners served as structuring agent, while Nomura Securities International and Mizuho Securities USA served as joint bookrunners.

The securitization also reflects ABL’s expanding geographic footprint, with collateral spanning the Northeast, Sun Belt, Rust Belt and South. The company said it plans to grow beyond its established Northeast presence and finance residential development in additional markets across the country.

Qatar Airways & Hans Zimmer Unveil “Burgundy Symphony” Sonic Identity

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Qatar Airways has introduced a new signature soundtrack, which can be heard on flights as of today. As someone who cares so much about airline boarding music that I keep a list of my favorite tunes, it’s something I’m both excited and sad about…

Qatar Airways has partnered with Oscar and Grammy award-winning composer Hans Zimmer for a new sonic identity for the airline, featuring a signature sound “that will elevate the passenger experience onboard and beyond.” This collaboration was first announced back in July 2025, but the music has now been released, as of November 2026.

You’re almost certainly familiar with Zimmer’s work, even if the name doesn’t ring a bell, as his music has been featured in everything from The Lion King, to Gladiator, to Pirates of the Caribbean, to The Da Vinci Code.

This new music will be played as the boarding and landing music on Qatar Airways flights, and will also be released as a full musical album on major streaming platforms, and across the carrier’s social channels.

The music is described as being “inspired by Qatar and its culture,” and it features a “Burgundy Symphony” album, which is a five-track album that encapsulates the airline’s wider sound identity and signature sound elements.

The album is described as capturing the rhythm of travel through five original tracks, each reflecting a different stage of the journey:

I can’t actually find all of these tracks online yet, though I’ll update this post with them if I do. The airline has released the below video, which I assume has one of the new tracks playing in the background.

As someone who loves airline boarding music, I’m of course delighted when airlines invest money into that aspect of the passenger experience (and my gosh, I can’t imagine how much money Qatar Airways is spending to get a whole album of Hans Zimmer music). It shows such an attention to detail that you don’t find on many airlines.

That being said, I adore Qatar Airways’ old boarding music, created by local Qatari composer Dana Al Fardan, and I just find it to be so incredibly calming and relaxing. The initial track was “The Awakening,” the second track was “The Dawn,” and the third track was “The Ascent.”

So I’m sad to hear that these will no longer be played, though at the same time, I’m sure the new track will be great as well. I can’t help but wonder if this development is almost a competitive response to airline startup Riyadh Air, which revealed its high profile brand sonic before even launching operations.

We know the two carriers are sort of going at one another, with Riyadh Air CEO Tony Douglas having stated that it’s “haram” that “the number one premium passenger for Qatar Airways, are Saudi nationals,” and Qatar Airways has responded with some pretty shady ads.

Qatar Airways has partnered with Hans Zimmer on its new sonic identity, including the introduction of a new boarding music track. As a self proclaimed connoisseur of boarding music, I’m very excited to see this collaboration, though I’ll miss Dana Al Fardan’s trilogy. Oh well, I guess I can always still listen to it at home!

What do you make of Qatar Airways partnering with Hans Zimmer?

Like Ben, I adore Riyadh Air’s signature music, and Saudia’s is great too. Unlike Ben, I also strongly prefer Etihad’s current music to Qatar’s. Beyond a point, Dana Al Fardan’s existing compositions seemed very trite to me, which contrasted to the premium feel of the cabin when I flew them several times in J recently. I have to reserve judgement until hearing the entire work that Hans Zimmer has done! (By the way, more recently I flew Finnair for the first time, and it also has introduced a dedicated list of compositions that reflect Nordic happiness.)

And more unforgettable scores by H. Zimmer: Batman Begins, The Dark Knight, The Dark Knight Rises, Dune, Dune 2, Inception, Interstellar, Rain Man, The Last Samurai, Pearl Harbor, Black Hawk Down, and The Prince of Egypt.

Dawn is when the fun is contagious because warm under the blankets . Qatar has nothing to do with it .

Arsenal vs Hull City preview: Gunners face draw specialists at the Emirates

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

Arsenal have the opportunity to strengthen their position in the Premier League table when they welcome Hull City to the Emirates Stadium on Saturday.

Mikel Arteta’s side secured a three-point lead after their 1-0 victory over Liverpool at Anfield and then followed it up with a 2-1 win in the Champions League against Slavia Prague on Wednesday.

The Gunners have won four games in a row but are looking to stretch their lead to six points ahead of Manchester City’s clash against Nottingham Forest later on Saturday. They are undefeated at home this season.

However, Hull will be looking to cause problems in North London.

Sergej Jakirovic’s side sit 11th after collecting 11 points from their opening nine league matches.

They have drawn five times, sharing the highest number of stalemates in the division. Three of their last four league fixtures have also ended level.

They drew 2-2 with Ipswich Town in their last game, but they needed to get back on track after falling behind. Recently they have failed to convert good performances into wins.

Despite Arsenal expecting to dominate possession, Hull’s 5-man defence may cause them problems.

The visitors have also had defensive issues recently, as they have failed to keep a clean sheet in their last seven games in all competitions. This may lead to chances being created for the Gunners attackers.

Arteta has to deal with his squad after the midweek trip to Prague. William Saliba is back in contention after recovering from his back injury but Cristhian Mosquera may stay in the squad if he is not rushed back in.

Ezri Konsa is a doubt after being withdrawn from Wednesday’s game with a knee problem. Bukayo Saka is also still sidelined as is Max Dowman.

Hull will be missing Matt Crooks who is suspended. Eliot Matazo, Matt Targett, Darko Gyabi, Ilyas Ansah and Charlie Hughes are also expected to miss out.

Raya; Timber, Mosquera, Gabriel, Calafiori; Guimarães, Rice; Madueke, Ødegaard, Tzolis; Havertz.

Tzolakis; Coyle, Ajayi, Egan, Mendy, Giles; Belloumi, Iroegbunam, Slater, Stroud; McBurnie.

Arsenal will face Hull City at the Emirates Stadium on Saturday, November 7. Kick off is at 3pm GMT.

Despite the fact that Hull has a tendency to draw, Arsenal remains a strong favorite to win this match. Arteta’s team will want to earn as many points as possible ahead of the international break.

Is Jobe Bellingham England's next big thing who shares his older brother's 'X-Factor' - or a 'problem child' whose dad blows up at his bosses? Inside the rise of the 21-year-old after first call-up

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 09:16 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 16:31 EDT, 6 November 2026

For years, Jobe Bellingham has been in the shadows of his older brother Jude.

Now, for the first time since they were children, the duo are poised to perform on the same stage. Only this time it won't be under the lampposts of Stourbridge, but the floodlights at Wembley.

Jobe, 21, has been called up to England's senior squad for the first time and is often described in similar terms to his elder sibling: relentlessly ambitious, focused, and a quick learner who is wise beyond his years.

'You have to have an X-Factor and Jobe does,' said Regis Le Bris, his old manager at Sunderland.

That X-Factor appears to run in the Bellingham blood. Earlier this week Jude was unveiled as the new underwear heart-throb for Calvin Klein, channelling his inner David Beckham in a smouldering photoshoot. No pressure, Jobe.

Their main difference on the pitch is that Jobe, while versatile, tends to occupy a deeper-lying role in midfield – and that actually increases the chances of Thomas Tuchel fielding them together. There is every chance that, against Croatia or Spain next week, we will see just the fourth pair of brothers to feature for England since the Second World War.

Jude and Jobe Bellingham's dream of playing for England together may finally come true

The duo grew up playing together and both joined the Birmingham City academy (Instagram: judebellingham)

The duo visited the Spanish Grand Prix in September with matching shirts and haircuts

They share a close relationship and in September were VIP guests of McLaren for the Spanish Grand Prix, posing for photographs in matching blue shirts and identical haircuts, a mischievous double act.

If you were a few pints deep in the Formula One hospitality area, you'd be forgiven for thinking they were the same bloke – especially when their footballing trajectories are so similar.

Jobe began his career at Birmingham City, like Jude, before moving to Sunderland and now Borussia Dortmund, where his brother also played. Jobe, who is two years younger than his superstar brother, is now in his second full season with the Westphalia giants.

He has previously admitted his trepidation at following in Jude's footsteps, once telling the German club's in-house media channel: 'I was biased against joining Borussia Dortmund for a very long time.

'I had anxieties, fears, and you want to be your own man. But I don't think I should let those stop me from making the right decision.'

The comparisons are inevitable, though, when you share the same name and your potential also happens to be through the ceiling. It is a theme Jobe will simply have to contend with wherever he roams.

In Dortmund's 2-2 draw with Bayern Munich on Saturday, the resemblance was striking as he delivered a standout performance – even having the ball in the back of the net, though his goal was ruled out for a Carney Chukwuemeka foul.

At one stage, Bellingham backheeled the ball over Aleksandar Pavlovic, won the ball back off Dayot Upamecano, drifted past Pavlovic with a skill that left him on one knee, and drove about 30 yards towards goal before firing a shot just over the bar.

They would be just the fourth pair of brother to play for England since the Second World War

Jude and Jobe are pictured together at a Louis Vuitton Paris Fashion Week show in 2023

In that ten-second flash, he displayed the sort of tenacity, ingenuity, and swagger which we have come to associate with Jude.

While not as brazen on the surface as Jude, Jobe perhaps gets some of his confidence from their father Mark.

The brothers were introduced to football by their dad, a retired police sergeant who also scored 700 goals in non-league.

Raising his sons in the West Midlands alongside their mother Denise, Mark gave them tips on how to improve and how to deal with the pressures of football. Jude even describes his father as his first footballing hero.

The crackerjack duo have always competed to see who can be the most precocious. Jude became Birmingham City's youngest-ever player when he debuted for them in 2019. When Jobe made his bow for them in 2022, he did so just 69 days older.

He earned his move to Sunderland after just 26 games and was a hit on Wearside. In his second campaign, he won the Championship Young Player of the Season award and helped them to promotion via the play-offs.

Manager Le Bris was breathless in his praise. 'There are many [qualities], there is a long list,' he said. 'Probably his ambition is one of the first ideas I have when I think about Jobe.

'He is very ambitious but this ambition is not naive. It is connected with what he is doing every day in training and every game on the pitch.

Jude pictured with his brother Jobe, father Mark, and mother Denise upon signing for Borussia Dortmund in 2020

'He has many good qualities as a footballer. He is very powerful and he understands the game very well. He is really interested in the game and, because of that, he is able to improve with experiences. He can learn very quickly.

'He is older than his age. He has more maturity. He is like an exception in the sport. It is not easy to play at the level he is doing so at his age, but he is doing so well.'

In the summer of 2025, Dortmund got on the blower. They were willing to pay £31million – more than they had for Jude – and put him straight in the squad for the Club World Cup. Dortmund's UK scout is Daniel Dodds, the brother of Mike, who had worked with Jobe in Birmingham's academy and at Sunderland.

'I think he can go to the very top,' Mike told the Northern Echo in 2023. 'Both brothers have a very similar mindset in terms of their relentlessness in their pursuit of where they want to get to.'

Despite the extravagant projections, Jobe's time in Germany's industrial belt has not been entirely smooth. Often he has been left to simmer on the bench, unable to nail down a regular starting spot.

In his first Klassiker experience, he came on only to lose the ball and have Michael Olise's shot deflect in off him. One report described him as a man who looked 'completely stripped of confidence'.

On the opening day of last season against St Pauli, he was hauled off at the break, leading to his father going ballistic and allegedly confronting sporting director Sebastian Kehl. That put Jobe in an awkward spot. He was also sent off for a professional foul against Freiburg.

Amid his struggles to adapt to the Bundesliga, he has copped criticism off the ferocious pundit Dietmar Hamann, who blasted him as a 'problem child' in a column for Sky Sport. 'Young, expensive and, so far, not even half as good as Jude Bellingham,' seethed the former Liverpool midfielder.

Jobe has become a more regular starter since the turn of the year and, in any case, has always been highly thought of in English football circles.

Jobe helped Sunderland to promotion from the Championship - manager Regis Le Bris said he had the 'X-factor'

Lee Carsley made Jobe his captain for the Under-21s and waxed lyrical about him: 'I think he will be an all-round box-to-box player. He can be creative, score goals. I still feel he can score more goals.

'His shooting is excellent, especially in training. He can link and receive in tight areas.'

Carsley added: 'He’s a good example to the rest of the group, the way he conducts himself on and off the pitch, the way he prepares, the influence he has on the rest of the team.

'He’s a player who can grow into that role. We just assume that players understand leadership and how that works. It will be good to add another string to his bow and keep improving his game.'

After his U21s debut against Spain in Cadiz, he was ambushed by people who wanted signatures... on their Real Madrid shirts. It was opportune and distasteful, but reflected the bigger picture: the name Bellingham is associated with Jude.

Now Jobe's moment has come. Tuchel picked him partly because of Carsley jabbering on with praise in his ear over recent months. The noise around him has become unavoidable and, with Alex Scott injured and Myles Lewis-Skelly suspended for the first game, the Three Lions are light in midfield.

'To play with my brother for England that would be the biggest dream of my life. Nothing would even get close to that,' Jude said in 2024. That may soon be a reality.

Goat Kari

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Food writer Rachel Gurjar made this goat kari for her “lifelong idol” Madhur Jaffrey, the renowned Indian cookbook author, at a dinner Gurjar hosted in her Brooklyn apartment in September 2022. When Jaffrey asked for the recipe, Gurjar knew she had to include it in her debut cookbook, East to East.

You can find goat meat at your local halal butcher or substitute lamb. If opting for goat, Gurjar recommends requesting a bone-in “curry cut,” which means the butcher will cut the meat into 2- to 3-inch pieces that are ideal for braising.

While you can use store-bought garam masala, Gurjar always DIYs her own spice blend inspired by the one her mother used to make back in India. “It’s the secret ingredient that often leaves my guests wondering why my Indian food tastes so exceptional,” she writes. “This version perfectly balances the warming notes of cloves and nutmeg, and it’s the one I return to time and time again.” You can also customize your blend: Add more cinnamon and fennel for a sweeter garam masala, or up the black pepper and cloves for a spicier kick.

Adapted from East to East: Recipes and Stories From Mumbai to New York by Rachel Gurjar. Copyright © 2026. Available from Chronicle Books.

Featured in “Keeping the Diwali Flame Burning in New York City.”

Chatpata Potato Chip Tower

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Cookbook author Rachel Gurjar doesn’t usually jump on food trends, but the potato chip tower called to her. “Chips are already great as is, but I knew a version inspired by chana chor—a fiery street snack made from flattened, fried black chickpeas tossed with onions, chiles, lemon, and chaat masala—would kick it up a notch,” she writes in her debut title, East to East. Chatpata is a Hindi word that means salty, sour, spicy, and sweet at the same time, and this chatpata potato chip tower absolutely lives up to its name. While you can use any potato chips you like—or even substitute tortilla chips, pita chips, or fried pita bread—Gurjar recommends thick kettle-cooked chips, as they stay crispy longer.

Adapted from East to East: Recipes and Stories From Mumbai to New York by Rachel Gurjar. Copyright © 2026. Available from Chronicle Books.

Matt Damon’s ship from ‘The Odyssey’ needs your help

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 2:15 PM EST

The lifesize wooden ship that took a beating during Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey is slated for some much-deserved TLC, and its owners are looking for volunteers to help. The 50-oar Draken Harald Hårfagre is scheduled for routine maintenance in the spring, and everyone is encouraged to apply online to enlist in the four-week endeavor—provided they can get to Norway by April 2027.

“A project like Draken depends not only on a professional crew and sailing deckhands, but also on dedicated volunteers who help support the life of the ship,” reads its official website. “Volunteering offers a hands-on way to contribute while learning more about the vessel, the work behind it, and the wider community that keeps the project moving forward.”

Nolan’s blockbuster adaptation of The Odyssey is a massive movie in every sense of the word, and a large part of the epic’s scale comes from its practical effects. Instead of falling back on computer-generated imagery, the award-winning director relied heavily on full-sized setpieces like an actual Trojan Horse. However, one of the film’s most prominent examples wasn’t actually built for the movie. For the role of Odysseus’ galley ship, Nolan enlisted the Draken.

The Draken technically isn’t period-accurate for The Odyssey’s Late Bronze Age setting. Then again, neither is the movie’s contemporary English dialogue, or choice of Matt Damon as the beleaguered king of Ithaca. The wooden ship is at least somewhat accurate by its own metrics. Commissioned in 2010 by Norwegian oil and gas magnate Sigurd Aase, the Draken is built with overlapping oak planks based in part on ship descriptions in historical Viking skaldic poems. At 114 feet long and 26 feet wide with a single, 79-foot-tall mast, the ship is ostensibly a scaled-up version of the famous Gokstad ship built around 890 CE.

The Draken’s maiden voyage took place in 2012, and a crew of 100 rowers successfully recreated the Vikings’ transatlantic crossing from Norway to Newfoundland only four years later. Today, it routinely sails to various ports around the world for exhibitions and educational tours—when it’s not guest starring in Hollywood blockbusters.

Regularly scheduled maintenance on the Draken will begin on April 10, 2027 and run through May 15. Jobs will include clinking the hull (replacing any weakened wooden planks) and scraping the exterior of ocean grime, as well as re-tarring, painting, and other general needs. Volunteers can sign up for any of the five, one-week windows,although the truly dedicated can elect to pitch in for the entire duration. Pretty much anyone over 18 years old is encouraged to apply, as long as they can get to Norway by next spring. Meals and accommodations are included, but preference will be given to volunteers with at least some knowledge of wooden ship maintenance. Once repairs are finished, the Draken will once again be ready for whatever next starring role comes its way.

National Nacho Day 2026: Here’s a list of deliciously cheesy freebies and deals

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Serious question: What do you call cheese that isn’t yours? Nacho cheese, of course.

Thankfully, the queso and more can be yours if you celebrate National Nacho Day, which is today (Friday, November 6, 2026). Before we get into where to get the best deals, let’s look at the history of this delicious dish.

A brief history of nachos

Nachos were an invention of necessity. In 1943, Ignacio “Nacho” Anaya García was employed at the Club Victoria restaurant in Piedras Negras, Mexico.

When some American military wives from just over the border in Eagle Pass, Texas, took a day trip, they stopped by the restaurant. Anaya wanted to offer them refreshments, so he fried some corn tortillas and topped them with cheese and jalapeños.

Nacho’s new dish was an instant hit.

He opened his own establishment, El Nacho, in the 1960s, which helped spread this new culinary delight. A recipe was published in 1954.

Nachos came to Los Angeles via El Cholo Restaurant and a waitress named Carmen Rocha.

A processed pourable cheese became a staple ingredient in the 1970s, thanks to a Texas businessman, Frank Liberto.

This forward thinker first sold nachos at Arlington Stadium, where baseball’s Texas Rangers once played. This action cemented the dish’s popularity with sports fans.

These days, nachos are as unique and versatile as the person who makes them.

National Nacho Day freebies and deals

It’s time to get even cheesier than our opening joke. Let’s see where we can chow down on some nachos.

At participating Moe’s Southwest Grill locations, rewards members can enjoy $5.99 nacho entrees beginning on Friday, November 6 (National Nacho Day) through Sunday, November 8.

On the big day at participating Las Palapas locations, the friendly staff wants you to have something to wash down your nachos with. This chain is offering 50% off on full orders of bean and cheese nachos and $5 regular margaritas.

Another half-off deal is going down at participating Condado Tacos locations. You can choose where you consume them; the offer is good if you dine in or order online. It even comes with free delivery if you order through November 8, spend at least $30, and use the Condado Tacos app. Talk about options.

If you find yourself near Nashville, head to El Comal Mexican Restaurant for 25% off all nachos.

In Pleasant Hill, California, east of Berkeley and Oakland, Cantina Jack’s is offering happy hour nacho pricing all day long.

However the tortilla chip crumbles, we hope it’s deliciously cheesy.

Innovation starts at the edges

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Most products, services, and systems are designed around a composite of the “average” user. That sounds logical. If you are trying to serve the largest possible market, why wouldn’t you design for the middle?

The dilemma is that the composite often fails to reflect how people actually live or experience the world. Those differences can expose where products, services, and systems work well, and where they do not.

Design innovation comes from many places. Accessibility should be one of them. It is a powerful lens, revealing friction, hidden assumptions, and opportunities that are easy to miss when we design only for the middle. Solutions developed for people who face the greatest barriers can make products better for the mainstream as well.

DESIGN AROUND CONSTRAINTS

Consider the familiar examples. Curb cuts were initially introduced to make sidewalks accessible to wheelchair users. They now also serve people pushing strollers, travelers with luggage, and workers moving carts. Closed captions are essential for people who are deaf or hard of hearing, and useful in noisy airports and quiet offices. Voice controls can be indispensable for someone with limited dexterity and convenient for anyone whose hands are occupied.

OXO, the maker of Good Grips kitchen utensils, offers a compelling business example. When founder Sam Farber saw his wife struggling to use a conventional vegetable peeler because of her arthritis, he recognized an opportunity to rethink an everyday product. The resulting Good Grips utensils—with their comfortable, easy-to-hold handles—addressed limited dexterity while appealing to consumers of all abilities. What began with one person’s difficulty became the foundation of a successful mainstream consumer brand.

These examples show how designing around constraints can lead to simpler, more intuitive solutions. The benefit need not stop with the people whose needs first inspired the design. A product that is easier to operate with one hand, or a service that eliminates unnecessary steps, can improve the experience for many more people. The implications extend beyond individual design decisions. Looking at familiar problems through the experiences of people whose needs have been overlooked can challenge conventional thinking, generate entirely new ideas, and reveal opportunities that might otherwise remain invisible.

Those opportunities may go well beyond improving existing products. When companies look closely at needs that are not being met, they may discover markets they have overlooked. What initially appears to be a narrow market can reveal broader demand and inspire new products, services, or business models. That is an opportunity to rethink what the mainstream market really includes, rather than assuming that existing customers represent everyone who might benefit.

A DIFFERENT APPROACH TO INNOVATION

Realizing that potential requires a different approach to innovation. Accessibility needs to influence the choices made at the beginning, when companies are defining the problem and imagining the solution. That means involving people with different needs and abilities in those early decisions, rather than asking them to assess a product after its basic design is settled. Their perspectives can challenge assumptions and reveal possibilities that might otherwise remain invisible. The aim is not to develop a separate product for every circumstance, but to build a richer understanding of what good design can accomplish.

There is another reason this matters now. Populations are aging. Expectations around independence and participation are changing. More customers expect products and services to fit the ways they live, rather than requiring them to adapt to rigid systems. What once seemed like a niche need may point to a much larger opportunity.

The edges of the market are moving closer to the center.

For business leaders, the choice is not between designing for accessibility and designing for the mainstream. That is a false distinction. The real opportunity is to make accessibility part of the thinking that shapes products and services from the outset. Meet needs that have too often been overlooked while creating something more useful for a broader population.

The best design begins by asking who has been left out of the design in the first place.

Gail Zauder is cofounder and CEO of AbleFly.

Without your audience in the room, your brand is guesswork

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A group of experienced executives is discussing how the brand can “connect with Gen Z.” Everyone has a deck and an opinion. But nobody in the room is Gen Z. I’ve sat in this type of meeting more times than I’d like to admit.

We collect demographic reports, analyze trends, and increasingly ask AI to tell us who our audiences are and what they want. But this goes wrong when we allow it to replace conversations.

A generation is not a community. A community is not an individual. People born in the same years can have almost nothing in common beyond their age. Demographics describe groups, not the individuals within them.

I’m more interested in what they choose and why. What do they dedicate their time to? Where do they gather? Why do they feel like they belong there? You cannot design your way into cultural relevance without first understanding the culture.

FEELINGS INSTEAD OF DATA

Pep Salazar has spent more than two decades learning this lesson from another perspective. As cofounder and director of OFFF, the international creativity and design festival that began in Barcelona over 25 years ago, he has helped turn a gathering of digital creatives into an enduring international community.

When we spoke recently, Pep described the tension between knowing an audience through metrics and understanding it as people. Sponsors want attendance numbers, engagement reports, and evidence of reach. Those figures do offer important insights, but they don’t explain why someone travels to Barcelona to spend three days among fellow creatives.

For him, it’s more interesting to understand feelings rather than data. A dashboard can tell you what people clicked. A conversation can tell you why they care.

Pep wants people to spend time together and talk. But his approach goes further. He also learns by taking risks. Pep deliberately introduces new perspectives and ideas that challenge what his audience already knows and expects. Not everything resonates immediately, but that’s part of the process. As he puts it, “I understand by doing.”

UNDERSTAND BY DOING

That’s an important distinction for brands. Understanding your audience doesn’t mean always giving people more of what they already know and love. It means knowing when to experiment and introducing something unexpected. And learning from the response.

When inviting a target audience into the process, you don’t have to outsource every decision to them. You can develop the judgment to understand when to respond to a community, and when to challenge it with something unfamiliar.

Pep also points to another common mistake brands make when trying to connect with creative communities: Don’t show up only to sell. He has watched brands arrive at OFFF with polished corporate messaging, while missing the social dynamics of the event. His advice is simple: “Come here to understand the people and to talk to them.”

LANGUAGE AND SOUL

He also worries that generative AI is making corporate language more interchangeable. “When you speak similar to your neighbor, then you don’t have a soul,” he told me.

I recognize that problem from my own work. Too many brands begin by studying the competition and end up borrowing its language, aesthetics, and assumptions. The result may look professional, but it rarely feels personal and unique. People want brands with personality that speak the language of their community.

So the next time people in a boardroom start debating how to reach a particular audience, ask who is missing in the room. Invite your audience in before the strategy is set in stone. Spend time where they gather. Make room for opinions and responses that might contradict your brief and give those perspectives the power to change the outcome.

Otherwise, the empty chair at the table says more about your brand than the strategy deck ever will.

Erik Herrström is founder and creative director of STUDIO HERRSTRÖM.

Why diagnostic delays cost more than time

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The cost of delayed diagnosis in rare disease is often measured in years. It should also be measured in dollars, clinical capacity, and missed opportunities for better care.

Rare disease affects 1 in 10 Americans, and on average, the diagnostic odyssey for a patient with rare disease costs over $220,000 in avoidable medical fees and lost income. It’s significant time and money that patients and families can’t afford to lose. And it doesn’t end there.

The downstream economic impacts are also costly. Every prolonged diagnosis can trigger a cascade of additional appointments, referrals, and procedures that consume resources. Rounds of tests, specialist bottlenecks, and administrative burden intensify the longer a patient goes without a diagnosis. This reduces clinical capacity and extends wait times for patient care.

Currently, patients often navigate years of uncertainty before accessing information that could meaningfully change their care. That model is increasingly difficult to justify.

This is why a genome-first healthcare system is so compelling. A genome-first approach, where whole genome sequencing (WGS) is used as a first-line test and not a last resort, is helping redefine personalized care for patients with rare diseases. For me, this is where the field is headed. Starting with the genome gives us a more complete view from the outset. When combined with other data, it may bring us closer to answers that many families have been waiting too long to receive. It’s about using technology with purpose to move care forward for patients seeking answers.

PUT COSTS INTO PERSPECTIVE

Here’s an example of how costs may be lowered with whole genome sequencing. Consider a six-year-old with a history of uncontrolled seizures and global developmental delay. A brain MRI and EEG were performed when the child was six months old and again at three years old. These did not provide answers about his symptoms. It wasn’t until he had WGS at age six that he tested positive for a genetic change that explained his symptoms. He has SCN1A-related seizure disorder. That diagnosis provided the clarity needed to personalize his treatment plan and transform his long-term care, including both medication and dietary changes.

In this example, the total estimated costs for just the main tests performed were $12,800. Repetitive testing costs can add up fast, and it can be particularly discouraging when the testing results are uninformative. Had WGS been offered earlier in this patient’s journey, the estimated savings would have been approximately $8,900 in testing costs alone. But we also can’t forget other costs not captured here, such as medications, specialist appointments, and potentially avoidable emergency visits and hospitalizations. These additional costs can compound quickly over time.  

THE WGS BENEFITS

Increasingly, healthcare systems should question if they can afford not to use WGS earlier.

The potential benefits of doing so in patient care are clear. It can help shorten the diagnostic odyssey, minimize repetitive or uninformative tests, enable more informed medical management, and lower long-term health costs. An earlier diagnosis can also inform targeted testing for family members to understand any symptoms they may have or their own risk for that condition.

For healthcare providers and health systems, WGS can improve operational efficiency by streamlining diagnostic workflows, ultimately helping clinicians make more informed decisions sooner and minimizing unnecessary testing rounds. In acute care settings, particularly neonatal and pediatric intensive care units, rapid WGS (rWGS) can accelerate clinical decision-making and shorten hospital stays. This can generate meaningful cost savings while improving patient outcomes.  

In fact, a recent study found that rWGS as a first-line test is likely to significantly reduce the cost of acute care in a private payer system. The analysis estimated a reduction of $5.8 million to $7.8 million in inpatient payments due to rWGS results and avoided inpatient days.

Every industry eventually reaches a point where a long-standing process no longer fits its purpose. Healthcare is approaching that moment with diagnostics for rare disease. A system built around sequential testing and years of uncertainty is increasingly being challenged by a model that starts with a more comprehensive understanding of the patient. I believe the organizations that embrace that shift will be better positioned to improve outcomes, reduce waste, and deliver a more efficient patient experience.

Kengo Takishima is chairman and chief executive officer of Baylor Genetics.

Denny’s store closures: See a list of shuttered locations as another franchisee files for bankruptcy

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The fate of dozens of Denny’s restaurants are hanging in the balance after a long-struggling franchisee for the beloved diner chain sought Chapter 11 protection on Wednesday, the latest in a string of bankruptcies involving a once-sprawling restaurant empire owned by a former McDonald’s executive.

The franchisee, EYM Diner, operated roughly 30 Denny’s restaurants at the time of its bankruptcy petition on Wednesday. A number of its locations had already closed in the lead-up to the filing, some quite recently.

Denny’s has been working behind the scenes to help keep the remaining restaurants open, the brand tells Fast Company.

EYM’s bankruptcy follows a lawsuit last month by one of its lenders, InterBank, which claims to be owed more than $18 million, and which has been pushing to have the restaurants sold to a more “capable” operator. 

Citing inspections from Denny’s, InterBank’s lawsuit documents an extensive period of alleged operational decline for EYM, claiming the conditions at its restaurants are “poor and getting worse,” including “food safety conditions at several locations that would likely not pass muster with the health department.”

“Denny’s cannot permit the operation of a substandard restaurant without damage to its brand,” the lawsuit states.

A lawyer for InterBank did not respond to a request for comment.

With restaurants located across Texas and Florida, EYM and its affiliates this week sought protection from bankruptcy court in the Northern District of Texas.

The franchise is owned by fast food entrepreneur Eduardo Diaz, a former president of McDonald’s Mexico, whose various companies have filed for multiple bankruptcies in recent years, Restaurant Business reported.

Diaz did not respond to requests for comments.

The embattled entrepreneur previously operated Pizza Hut, KFC, and Panera Bread locations, including several that reopened this year under new ownership, as Fast Company previously reported.

Mark Burgess, Denny’s chief development officer, told Fast Company in a statement that the diner chain has worked with Diaz for two years to try sell the restaurants. That effort is still ongoing.

“There are viable restaurants in this group, and franchise partners within our system are willing to step in to help secure their future,” Burgess said.

Which Denny’s restaurants have closed? 

According to a franchise disclosure document issued by Denny’s in April, Diaz operated 39 Denny’s locations as of the end of last year.

Some of those stores have since shuttered. A Fast Company review of the addresses revealed at least eight Denny’s locations affiliated with Diaz that have been marked as closed on review platforms or delivery services.

Texas

  • 2655 Grapevine Mills Cir E, Grapevine, TX 76051
  • 510 W Lyndon B Johnson Fwy, Irving, TX 75063
  • 6480 N Fwy, Fort Worth, TX 76137

Florida

  • 3701 Bee Ridge Rd, Sarasota, FL 34233
  • 5603 E Hillsborough Ave, Tampa, FL 33610 
  • 1245 N Atlantic Ave, Cocoa Beach, FL 32931
  • 255 E Altamonte Dr, Altamonte Springs, FL 32701
  • 4390 US-27, Sebring, FL 33870

The locations appear to have closed recently, with reviews for some of the stores appearing as recently as this week.

As of Friday, the locations have either been removed from Denny’s store locator tool, have been marked as not accepting orders, or have phone numbers that appear to have been disconnected.

It was not immediately clear if any of the locations could reopen at some point.

Specific Denny’s closures were not mentioned in EYM’s bankruptcy petition, though a typical course of action would be for the company to ask for court permission to get out of its lease obligations following the bankruptcy.

The above list of store closures may not be complete. We’ll update this story if we learn of more.

Why does all this sound familiar?

This is not the first franchise bankruptcy to impact Denny’s in recent months. In September, franchise owner M15 Inc. closed several Denny’s restaurants in Minnesota and Wisconsin in the lead-up to a Chapter 7 bankruptcy filing.

Denny’s Corporation, which was taken private and delisted from the Nasdaq earlier this year after years of struggles, is now in the hands of private equity backers. As of the end of last year, the chain had 1,274 locations, most of which were franchise-owned.

Stellar Blade On Switch 2 Looks Like A Great Port, Until You Start Fighting

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There's no denying that Stellar Blade is one seriously good-looking game. On Switch 2, it runs surprisingly well on Nintendo's hybrid system, and it'd be a perfect port if it wasn't for one glaring issue that holds it back.

On the surface, Stellar Blade looks like a smooth port with few compromises, as it still offers plenty of graphical detail at a lower resolution. Stellar Blade on Switch 2 also targets 60fps, but in order to achieve that, it uses a very aggressive form of frame generation to create a smooth sense of action. The problem with this is that it makes the game harder to play, because your actions are delayed.

Parries and dodges require perfect timing, and because the game is delayed, pressing a button right as an enemy hits you is often too late, causing you to take damage. This also means that controlling the protagonist Eve feels somewhat sluggish, navigating the environments feels sluggish, and during chaotic fights, the game can lag so badly that it feels like your controller is completely ignoring your inputs. This is at odds with how the game teaches you to master its gameplay, as the natural rhythm you're supposed to pick up alongside visual cues simply does not match up with the delicate timing needed to pull off counters and parries.

Because Stellar Blade is an action game that's tightly centered on strict frame windows for counters and dodges--think of it as being soulslike-adjacent with its combat--the disconnect between the 60fps visuals and the heavier 30fps input feel makes precise timing noticeably harder to hit.

Developer Shift Up was aware of this, as after it received player feedback from the Stellar Blade Switch 2 demo, it added a Frame Generation Toggle to the game's settings. Turning it off will make the game run at a visually native frame rate, and it removes the extra buffer, which goes a long way toward making controller responsiveness feel tighter. Your mileage may vary, and if you can overcome the unnatural timing behind defensive moves in Stellar Blade on Switch 2, there's plenty to love about it.

But if you have a decent PC or a PS5? Stellar Blade plays much better on those platforms, even if you can't take them on the go with you. The Switch 2 edition does include some nice extras like Bayonetta outfits and all of the post-launch content, so if you're eyeing a purchase of Stellar Blade on it, grab the demo and see if you can get to grips with how the game plays on that platform.

Shift Up is also working on a sequel, Stellar Blade: Blood Rain, which features a new pint-sized powerhouse of a protagonist.

Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Remake Involved Xenoblade Chronicles Creator Monolith Soft

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

We should acknowledge that Monolith Soft isn't just a Xenoblade developer, but also a major supporter of The Legend of Zelda franchise. The studio confirmed on X that it assisted with programming and graphics (per a machine translation) on the Ocarina of Time remake. This marks the fifth time the team has contributed to a mainline Zelda title, starting with 2011's Skyward Sword. 

There were a lot of theories in the past couple of months about what team was behind The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time on Switch 2--especially considering the visual overhaul and the Internet's reaction. Earlier this week, Nintendo revealed that its internal team responsible for Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom, Entertainment Planning & Development (EPD) Production Group No. 3, was responsible for the remake. We now know that Monolith Soft also pitched in, as the team did with the two Switch mainline entries, too.

In 2007, Nintendo acquired Monolith Soft from Bandai Namco, with the developer at the time working on Disaster: Day of Crisis for Wii. The studio was probably best known for the Xenosaga series, but it's doubtful anyone could predict how prolific Monolith Soft would become over the past 19 years. 

Just looking at Monolith Soft's portfolio, the developer has already contributed to three Switch 2 heavy hitters: Ocarina of Time, Splatoon Raiders, and Mario Kart World. This is all in addition to overseeing and creating the Xenoblade franchise, with Xenoblade Genesis planned for release next year. It's an impressive resume. 

Zelda: Ocarina of Time producers Eiji Aonuma and Hidemaro Fujibayashi recently chatted with GameSpot about the game's development. The Switch 2 exclusive released yesterday, November 5, and is already wearing out the screenshot button for Steve Watts. You can see how gorgeous the remake is compared to the Nintendo 64 original.

Blue Origin Announces New Glenn Third Stage, New Booster Engine
